. The proof is similar to the bounded version (Lemma
3.3
) except we just add an intersection with
B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})
where
\Upsilon_{1}
is the chosen root node from the countable packing
S_{m}
, and replace
d
with
d_{m}
everywhere.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
[\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2},\dots,]
be the nodes of an infinite path in the graph
G
, i.e.,
\Upsilon_{J+1}\in\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{J})
for all
J\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for any integers
J\geq J^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\|\Upsilon_{J^{\prime}}-\Upsilon_{J}\|\leq\frac{d_{m}(2+4c)}{c2^{J^{\prime}}}
.

Finally, we may now describe the actual estimator. In the edge case where
S=\varnothing
, our estimator is the lexicographically smallest point in the set
S(\hat{R})
where
\hat{R}=\min_{t>R}\{t:S(t)\neq\varnothing\}
.
4
4

4

We have a
\min
here because the sets
S(t)
are nested and closed; hence for any monotone decreasing sequence
t_{i}\rightarrow t_{0}
, if each of the sets
S(t_{i})
is non-empty, then
S(t_{0})=\cap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}S(t_{i})
is also non-empty.
We will return to this edge case later.

Otherwise, if
S\neq\varnothing
, we proceed with a similar iterative estimation procedure. That is, let the random variable
\Upsilon_{1}\in S_{m}
be the point in the
m
-packing
S_{m}
that is closest to the random set
S
, breaking ties lexicographically. Note that one of the directed trees that we constructed has a root at
\Upsilon_{1}
. Observe that
S
is contained in
B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})\cap K
. To see this: if
\Upsilon_{1}\in S
, then since
\mathrm{diam}(S)\leq 2R<d_{m}
, clearly
S\subset B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})\cap K
. If
\Upsilon_{1}\not\in S
, there exists some
s\in S
such that
\|\Upsilon_{1}-s\|\leq 2m
since
\Upsilon_{1}
is the closest point in the
2m
-covering of
K
(which contains
S
). But for any
s^{\prime}\in S
, we have
\|s-s^{\prime}\|\leq 2R
by the diameter result, hence
\|\Upsilon_{1}-s^{\prime}\|\leq d_{m}
by the triangle inequality. Thus,
S\subset B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})
. So by Lemma
5.3
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mu\in B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})\cap K|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}(\mu\in S|S\neq\varnothing)\geq\mathbb{P}(\mu\in S)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma R^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right),

(5.3)

where we used that
\{\mu\in S\}\subset\{S\neq\varnothing\}
to drop the conditioning.

Now, apply Algorithm
2
to the directed tree rooted at
\Upsilon_{1}
to obtain a sequence
[\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2},\dots]
. As before, we use
d_{m}=2m+2R
in place of
d
. We use our modified directed tree properties in Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
. We still have the Cauchy sequence property from Lemma
5.8
except with
d_{m}
. Our previous tournament bound in Lemma
3.7
still holds. Then we need some modified auxiliary lemmas for the upper bound. From now on, we must separately consider the Gaussian and sub-Gaussian cases, but all that changes is swapping out the condition on
\delta
. We re-use the same (absolute) constants from either bounded case, e.g.,
C_{1}(\kappa),C_{3}(\kappa)
in the Gaussian case.

Theorem 5.9
.

Consider the Gaussian noise setting. Let
c=2(C+1)>2
be the constant used in the local metric entropy. For any
J\geq 1
, define
\eta_{J}=\frac{d_{m}\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}
where
C_{3}(\kappa)
is from Theorem
3.6
and
d_{m}=2m+2R
with
m=\tfrac{R}{c-1}
(and
R
chosen as in (
5.1
)). Let
J^{\ast}
be the maximal
J
such that

\frac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}>2\log\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\tfrac{c\eta_{J}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c\right)\right]^{3}\vee\log 2,

(5.4)

and set
J^{\ast}=1
if this condition never occurs. If
S(R)\neq\varnothing
, write
\nu^{\ast\ast}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
as the output of at least
J^{\ast}
iterations of Algorithm
2
. If
S(R)=\varnothing
, write
\nu^{\ast\ast}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
as
\hat{p}
, where
\hat{p}
is the lexicographically smallest point in
S(\hat{R})
defined previously. Then we have
\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\nu^{\ast\ast}-\mu\|^{2}\lesssim\max\left(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\right)
.

We proceed to prove our algorithm in the unbounded Gaussian setting attains the minimax lower bound. While we do not state it formally, our symmetric or known sub-Gaussian noise setting will have a similar guarantee. This is due to our Remark
4.3
.

Theorem 5.10

(Robust Unbounded Gaussian Minimax Rate)
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
. Define

\eta^{\ast}=\sup\{\eta\geq 0:N\eta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta,c)\}.

Then for sufficiently large
c
, the minimax rate is
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2})
.

We now state the (general) sub-Gaussian equivalents of the unbounded algorithm error. Note also that we reset our absolute constants from the Gaussian section, and we re-use the absolute constants from the bounded, sub-Gaussian setting.

Theorem 5.11
.

Consider the sub-Gaussian noise setting where
\epsilon\in(0,1/32)
. Let
c=2(C+1)
be the constant used in the local metric entropy. For any
J\geq 1
, define
\eta_{J}=\frac{d_{m}\sqrt{C_{5}}}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}
where
C_{5}
is from Theorem
4.5
and
d_{m}=2m+2R
with
m=\tfrac{R}{c-1}
and
R
chosen as in (
5.1
). Let
J^{\ast}
be the maximal
J
such that

\frac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}>2\log\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\frac{c\eta_{J}}{\sqrt{C_{5}}},2c\right)\right]^{3}\vee\log 2,

(5.5)

and set
J^{\ast}=1
if this condition never occurs. If
S(R)\neq\varnothing
, we set
\nu^{\ast\ast}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
as the output of at least
J^{\ast}
iterations of Algorithm
2
. If
S(R)=\varnothing
, set
\nu^{\ast\ast}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
as
\hat{p}
, where
\hat{p}
is the lexicographically smallest point in
S(\hat{R})
defined previously. Then we have
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\nu^{\ast\ast}-\mu\|^{2}\lesssim\max\left(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\sigma^{2}\right)
.

Theorem 5.12

(Robust Unbounded Sub-Gaussian Minimax Rate)
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1/32)
. Define

\eta^{\ast}=\sup\{\eta\geq 0:N\eta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta,c)\}.

Then for sufficiently large
c
, the minimax rate is
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon))
.

Remark 5.13
.

In this remark, we show that if
K
is not closed, the minimax rate obtained by Theorems
5.10
and
5.12
remains valid even though we used
\overline{K}
in place of
K
in our algorithms. To that end, we will first argue that
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\overline{K}}(\eta,c)\leq\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{K}(\tfrac{\eta(2c+2)}{2c+1},2c)
. To see this, assume that
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\overline{K}}(\eta,c)
is achieved at a point
\nu\in\overline{K}
(if the supremum is not achieved, then we can take a limiting sequence and repeat the same steps for that sequence). For brevity, denote
M=\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\overline{K}}(\eta,c)
, and let
\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{M}\}\subset\overline{K}\cap B(\nu,\eta)
be an
\eta/c
-packing. Observe that since
\nu,\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{M}\in\overline{K}
we can find points from
K
arbitrarily close to any of these points. Suppose that
\nu^{\prime},\nu_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\nu_{M}^{\prime}\in K
are such that
\|\nu-\nu^{\prime}\|\vee\max_{i\in[M]}\|\nu_{i}-\nu^{\prime}_{i}\|\leq\kappa
for some small
\kappa>0
. Now we claim that
\nu_{i}^{\prime}\in B(\nu^{\prime},\eta+2\kappa)\cap K
and that the
\nu_{i}^{\prime}
form a
(\tfrac{\eta}{c}-2\kappa)
-packing of this set. Both claims follow from the triangle inequality. Since
\kappa
is arbitrary, we can select
\kappa=\tfrac{\eta}{4c+2}
and prove the claimed local entropy inequality.

Hence

\displaystyle\eta^{*}

\displaystyle=\sup\{\eta\geq 0:N\eta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq\log\mathcal{M}_{\overline{K}}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta,c)\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\{\eta\geq 0:N\eta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{\eta(2c+2)}{2c+1},2c)\}=:\hat{\eta}.

Recall Theorems
5.10
and
5.12
argued that the worst case rate of our algorithm is
\lesssim\max(\eta^{*2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2})
in the Gaussian case (and known or symmetric sub-Gaussian noise) and
\lesssim\max(\eta^{*2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log 1/\epsilon)
in the unknown sub-Gaussian noise case. Next, by Lemma 1.5 of
Prasadan and Neykov (2024)
,
\hat{\eta}
(defined above) satisfies:

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}\asymp\sup\{\eta\geq 0:N\eta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta,2c)\}=:\tilde{\eta}.

Note that Lemma 1.5 of that paper uses the monotonicity property of local metric entropy of convex sets, but we showed in Lemma
1.4
this property holds in the star-shaped setting.
Thus we conclude that the worst case rate of our procedure is upper bounded by
\lesssim\max(\tilde{\eta}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2})
in the Gaussian case (and in the known or symmetric sub-Gaussian case) and
\lesssim\max(\tilde{\eta}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log 1/\epsilon)
in the unknown sub-Gaussian noise case. On the other hand,
\tilde{\eta}
is a valid lower bound by the argument in Section
5.1
, as are
\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}
and
\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)
for the Gaussian and sub-Gaussian settings, respectively.

5.3
Example: Sparse Robust Mean Estimation

We close our paper with an application to the well-studied setting of sparse mean estimation and capture the impact of adversarial corruption. In this setting, the statistician does not know which predictors are irrelevant, and the adversary could in principle try to confuse the statistician by zeroing out the ‘wrong’ ones.

Let
K
be the set of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
where it is known at most
s
of the coordinates are non-zero for some integer
1\leq s\leq n/8
. This is clearly an unbounded star-shaped set with the
0
vector as the center.

Lemma 5.14
.

\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta,c)\asymp s\log(1+\tfrac{n}{2s})
.

As a consequence, in the Gaussian (or symmetric or known sub-Gaussian) noise setting, applying Theorem
5.10
, we have
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\asymp\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta^{\ast},c)\asymp\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\cdot s\log(1+\tfrac{n}{2s})
. The minimax rate is thus given by

\max\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\cdot s\log(1+\tfrac{n}{2s}),\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\right).

Similarly, in the unknown sub-Gaussian setting, the minimax rate is

\max\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\cdot s\log(1+\tfrac{n}{2s}),\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\right)

by Theorem
5.12
. Our algorithm in all cases achieves the minimax rate, although recall that we must have knowledge of
\sigma^{2}
in this unbounded setting as well as the corruption rate
\epsilon
and the sparsity
s
.

Several comments are in order about the novelty of the above result. When there are no outliers present with known
\sigma
, the above result was first established by
Donoho et al. (1992)
. When outliers are present, there are multiple previous related works, including
(
Balakrishnan et al., 2017
;
Diakonikolas et al., 2019b
;
Cheng et al., 2022
;
Diakonikolas et al., 2022
)
. The closest result to our characterization above is Theorem 88 of
Diakonikolas et al. (2022)
which states that under the case of Gaussian noise, if
N\gtrsim(s\log n)/\epsilon^{2}
the minimax rate is
\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}
in high probability. Observe that even in this simple Gaussian case, this result fails to capture the full landscape of the rate.

6
Discussion and Future Work

In this paper, we presented a minimax optimal estimation procedure for the adversarially corrupted (sub)-Gaussian location model with star-shaped constraints. Our results were inspired by the work of
Neykov (2022)
on the minimax rate of the uncontaminated, convex constrained Gaussian sequence model. The proofs presented in this manuscript use deep results from probability theory (such as local CLTs) to obtain the first definitive robust minimax rate for all sub-Gaussian distributions under any kind of star-shaped constraint, regardless of whether the noise distribution is known or unknown. Interestingly, as it turns out, if one knows the sub-Gaussian noise distribution, the minimax optimal rate is faster than if the distribution is unknown. The obvious drawback of this work is that the algorithms are hard to implement, but we do hope our paper inspires efforts to devise computationally efficient algorithms achieving the same performance under various constraints for the mean.

We now comment on the i.i.d. Gaussian case. We believe that our results can be used to understand what happens with the minimax rate when
\kappa\rightarrow 0
, that is, the number of outliers approaches
1/2
. In a recent paper by
Kotekal and Gao (2025)
, the authors demonstrated (see Appendix E therein), that the minimax rate for the problem when
K=\mathbb{R}^{1}
is
\sigma^{2}\log(\tfrac{en}{n(1-2\epsilon)})
when
\epsilon
approaches
1/2
. We think that by carefully studying the behavior of the constant
C_{1}
in the proof of our Theorem
3.6
, and more specifically Lemma
B.2
, and some of our results in Section
5
, one can obtain a similar result for the much more general multivariate case under constraints. We leave the details of this exercise for future exploration.

Another avenue for future research is to understand the case when the noise can be heavy tailed, e.g., it is only assumed that
\sup_{v\in S^{n-1}}\mathbb{E}(v{{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\xi_{i})^{2}\leq D
for some
D>0
. We believe that our ideas can be useful for deriving the information-theoretic limits of this setting as well. However, the challenge there is that we no longer have simple tail bounds for heavy-tailed random variables as opposed to the sub-Gaussian variables considered in this work. Yet another potential exploration is using Lepski’s method to make our algorithm adaptive to unknown
\epsilon
in the unknown sub-Gaussian case (by contrast, our Gaussian case permits unknown
\epsilon
).

7
Acknowledgements

The authors appreciate the input of an Associate Editor and two anonymous referees for suggestions that fixed typos and greatly improved the presentation of the manuscript.

Appendix A
Proofs for Section
2

To prove our uncorrupted lower bound, we first state Fano’s inequality and subsequently prove a bound on the mutual information.

Lemma A.1

(Fano’s inequality)
.

Let
\mu^{1},\ldots,\mu^{m}
be a collection of
\eta
-separated points in the parameter space in Euclidean norm. Suppose
J
is uniformly distributed over the index set
[m]
, and for each
i\in[N]
and
j\in[m],
we have
(\tilde{X}_{i}|J=j)=\mu^{j}+\xi_{i}
where
\xi_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}\sigma^{2})
. Let the mutual information
I(\tilde{X};J)
be defined as the average Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence of the
\prod_{i}(\tilde{X}_{i}|J=j)
and their mixture across
J
as defined in
Wainwright [2019, Equation(15.30)]
. Then

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\mu}}\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu\|^{2}\geq\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}\bigg(1-\frac{I(\tilde{X};J)+\log 2}{\log m}\bigg).

Lemma A.2

(Bounding the Mutual Information)
.

For any
\nu\in K
we have

I(\tilde{X};J)\leq\frac{N}{2\sigma^{2}}\max_{j}\|\mu^{j}-\nu\|^{2}.

Proof.

We use
Wainwright [2019, equation (15.52)]
, the independence of
\tilde{X}_{1},\dots,\tilde{X}_{n}
, as well as properties of KL-divergence
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
for Gaussian random variables.
Let
\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{X}_{i}}^{\mu^{j}}
be the distribution of
(\tilde{X}_{i}|J=j)
, which is
\mathcal{N}(\mu^{j},\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})
, and let
\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{X}_{i}}^{\nu}
be the distribution of
\tilde{X}_{i}=\nu+\xi_{i}
which is
\mathcal{N}(\nu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})
. Then we obtain

\displaystyle I(\tilde{X};J)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\prod_{i}\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{X}_{i}}^{\mu^{j}}\parallel\prod_{i}\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{X}_{i}}^{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j}\sum_{i}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{X}_{i}}^{\mu^{j}}\parallel\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{X}_{i}}^{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j}\sum_{i}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{N}(\mu^{j},\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})\parallel\mathcal{N}(\nu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}m}\sum_{j}\sum_{i}\|\mu^{j}-\nu\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2\sigma^{2}m}\sum_{j}\|\mu^{j}-\nu\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{N}{2\sigma^{2}}\max_{j}\|\mu^{j}-\nu\|^{2}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

The first inequality is obvious, since if the adversary does nothing to the original observations, we obtain the middle term. We now show the second inequality. Pick a
\nu\in K
that achieves

M:=\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta,c):=\sup_{\nu\in K}\mathcal{M}(\eta/c,B(\nu,\eta)\cap K)

(otherwise, repeat the following argument by picking a limiting sequence of functions). Let
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{M}
be a maximal
\eta/c
-packing set for
B(\nu,\eta)\cap K
, so that
\|\nu_{i}-\nu_{j}\|>\eta/c
for all
i\neq j
. Because
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{M}\in B(\nu,\eta)\cap K
, it follows for each
j\in[M]
that
\|\nu_{j}-\nu\|\leq\eta
. Letting
J
be uniformly distributed on
[M]
, we have from Lemma
A.2
that

\displaystyle I(\tilde{X};J)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{N}{2\sigma^{2}}\cdot\max_{j}\|\nu_{j}-\nu\|^{2}\leq\tfrac{N\eta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}.

Applying Lemma
A.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\mu}}\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\eta^{2}}{4c^{2}}\bigg(1-\frac{\frac{N\eta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\log 2}{\log M}\bigg).

Choose
\eta
such that
\log M
is bigger than the middle term in

\log M\geq 4\left(\frac{N\eta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\vee\log 2\right)\geq 2\left(\frac{N\eta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\log 2\right),

and this proves the minimax risk is lower-bounded by
\frac{\eta^{2}}{8c^{2}}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

First, take
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2N}}<1/2-\kappa
and observe that
\epsilon^{\prime}\asymp\epsilon
. Next, pick
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in K
such that

(d/3)^{2}\wedge\tfrac{4\sigma^{2}{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1-\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}\leq\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|^{2}\leq\tfrac{4\sigma^{2}{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1-\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}},

which we can do by the star-shaped property in Lemma
1.3
.

Now consider the two Gaussian measures
\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta_{1},\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I}_{p})
and
\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta_{2},\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I}_{p})
, and observe that
\operatorname{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}})\leq\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}
. This follows since
\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\sigma^{2}(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{(1-\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}
by Pinsker’s inequality, which states that

\operatorname{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}})\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}}\|\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}})}.

Following
Chen et al. [2018]
, we can pick
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
such that
\operatorname{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}})=\frac{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}{1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}
since
\operatorname{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}})\in(0,\tfrac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}]
. Write
p_{\theta_{1}}
and
p_{\theta_{2}}
as the densities of
\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure and define the distributions
Q_{1},Q_{2}
with densities
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
by

\displaystyle q_{1}=\frac{(p_{\theta_{2}}-p_{\theta_{1}})\mathbbm{1}(p_{\theta_{2}}\geq p_{\theta_{1}})}{\operatorname{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}})},~~~~~~q_{2}=\frac{(p_{\theta_{1}}-p_{\theta_{2}})\mathbbm{1}(p_{\theta_{1}}\geq p_{\theta_{2}})}{\operatorname{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}})}.

One can verify (see
Chen et al. [2018]
for a proof) that
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are indeed probability densities. Now consider the mixture measures
R_{1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})P_{\theta_{1}}+\epsilon^{\prime\prime}Q_{1}
and
R_{2}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})P_{\theta_{2}}+\epsilon^{\prime\prime}Q_{2}
with densities
r_{1},r_{2}
. Using that
\operatorname{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{2}})=\frac{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}{1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}
, their densities with respect to the Lebesgue measure are:

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})p_{\theta_{1}}+(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})(p_{\theta_{2}}-p_{\theta_{1}})\mathbbm{1}(p_{\theta_{2}}\geq p_{\theta_{1}})

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})p_{\theta_{1}}\mathbbm{1}(p_{\theta_{1}}\geq p_{\theta_{2}})+(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})p_{\theta_{2}}\mathbbm{1}(p_{\theta_{2}}\geq p_{\theta_{1}})

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})(p_{\theta_{1}}-p_{\theta_{2}})\mathbbm{1}(p_{\theta_{1}}\geq p_{\theta_{2}})+(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})p_{\theta_{2}}

\displaystyle=r_{2}.

Hence the two measures
R_{1},R_{2}
are the same and
\operatorname{TV}(R_{1}^{\otimes N},R_{2}^{\otimes N})=0.

We now expand the TV distance
\operatorname{TV}(R_{1}^{\otimes N},R_{2}^{\otimes N})
. Let us define some useful notation. Define
\binom{[N]}{s}=\{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}):i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\in[N],i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots i_{s}\}
. Define the quantities

\displaystyle S_{1}^{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})}\sum_{I\in\binom{[N]}{s}}(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{N-s}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{s}\prod_{i\in[N]\setminus I}p_{\theta_{1}}(x_{i})\prod_{i\in I}q_{1}(x_{i})

\displaystyle S_{2}^{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})}\sum_{I\in\binom{[N]}{s}}(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{N-s}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{s}\prod_{i\in[N]\setminus I}p_{\theta_{2}}(x_{i})\prod_{i\in I}q_{2}(x_{i})

\displaystyle S_{1}^{U}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})+1}^{N}\sum_{I\in\binom{[N]}{s}}(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{N-s}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{s}\prod_{i\in[N]\setminus I}p_{\theta_{1}}(x_{i})\prod_{i\in I}q_{1}(x_{i})

\displaystyle S_{2}^{U}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})+1}^{N}\sum_{I\in\binom{[N]}{s}}(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{N-s}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{s}\prod_{i\in[N]\setminus I}p_{\theta_{2}}(x_{i})\prod_{i\in I}q_{2}(x_{i}),

assuming the summation bounds are integers for simplicity. By applying the triangle inequality and integrating out the densities in each of the
S_{i}^{U}
terms, so that one is left with terms of the form
\sum_{s=N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})+1}^{N}\binom{N}{s}(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{s}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{N-s}
, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\int|S_{1}^{U}-S_{2}^{U}|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})>N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})\right).

(A.1)

Then expanding out the product of densities, and then splitting the sum into
S_{1}^{L},S_{2}^{L}
and
S_{1}^{U},S_{2}^{U}
with the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{TV}(R_{1}^{\otimes N},R_{2}^{\otimes N})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\bigg|\prod_{i\in[N]}[(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})p_{\theta_{1}}(x_{i})+\epsilon^{\prime\prime}q_{1}(x_{i})]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\prod_{i\in[N]}[(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})p_{\theta_{2}}(x_{i})+\epsilon^{\prime\prime}q_{2}(x_{i})]\bigg|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\bigg|\sum_{s=0}^{N}\sum_{I\in\binom{[N]}{s}}(1-\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{N-s}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})^{s}\Big[\prod_{i\in[N]\setminus I}p_{\theta_{1}}(x_{i})\prod_{i\in I}q_{1}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\prod_{i\in[N]\setminus I}p_{\theta_{2}}(x_{i})\prod_{i\in I}q_{2}(x_{i})\Big]\bigg|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int|S_{1}^{L}-S_{2}^{L}|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\frac{1}{2}\int|S_{1}^{U}-S_{2}^{U}|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int|S_{1}^{L}-S_{2}^{L}|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})>N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int|S_{1}^{L}-S_{2}^{L}|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\exp(-1)

where we used (
A.1
) and Hoeffding’s inequality in last two steps. Since the TV is 0, we have
\frac{1}{2}\int|S_{1}^{L}-S_{2}^{L}|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq\exp(-1)
. Note also that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\leq N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})\right)\geq 1-\exp(-1).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\tfrac{1}{2}\int|S_{1}^{L}-S_{2}^{L}|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}{\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\leq N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N}))}\leq\frac{\exp(-1)}{1-\exp(-1)}<1.

Now, upon further inspection, one can see that the left-hand side is the
\mathrm{TV}
between conditional mixture measures defined as follows: Given
N
observations of
R_{1}
, let
W_{1}
be the number of times
Q_{1}
is drawn (in place of
P_{\theta_{1}}
), which is a
\mathrm{Bin}(N,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})
random variable. Define
\tilde{R}_{1}=R_{1}^{\otimes N}|\{W_{1}\leq N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})\}
. Similarly, let
W_{2}
be the number of times
Q_{2}
is drawn (in place of
P_{\theta_{2}}
) in
N
observations of
R_{2}
, and define
\tilde{R}_{2}=R_{2}^{\otimes N}|\{W_{2}\leq N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})\}
. In other words, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\tilde{R}_{i}
is the distribution
P_{\theta_{i}}^{\otimes N}
conditional on
N(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+1/\sqrt{2N})
(which is
\leq N\epsilon
) of the observations corrupted into having distribution
Q_{i}
. We have just shown
\mathrm{TV}(\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{R}_{2})<1
.

Thus, by Le Cam’s two point lemma
[
Wainwright, 2019
, Chapter 15]
we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\mu}}\sup_{\mu\in K}\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|\hat{\mu}(C(\tilde{X}))-\mu\|^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}(1-\mathrm{TV}(\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{R}_{2}))

\displaystyle\gtrsim\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\wedge d^{2}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.

Let
\mu
be the center of
K
(from the definition of a star-shaped set). Consider drawing data from a mixture distribution between a Gaussian and a constant, i.e.,
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}
where
\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}=(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\cdot\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})+(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\nu
. We require the mean
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}:=(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\mu+(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\nu
to be in
K
, while not necessarily requiring
\nu\in K
.

Let us first ensure the distribution is sub-Gaussian. We equivalently characterize
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}
as follows: for a
\mathrm{Ber}(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})
random variable
W_{i}
, let
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})
when
W_{i}=0
and
X_{i}=\nu
when
W_{i}=1
. For any
t\geq 0
and any unit vector
v\in S^{n-1}
, observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(v^{T}(X_{i}-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}})\geq t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(W_{i}=0)\mathbb{P}\left(v^{T}(X_{i}-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}})\geq t|W_{i}=0\right)+

\displaystyle\quad\quad\mathbb{P}(W_{i}=1)\mathbb{P}\left(v^{T}(X_{i}-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}})\geq t|W_{i}=1\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\mathbb{P}\left(v^{T}(\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})-(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\mu-(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\nu)\geq t\right)+

\displaystyle\quad\quad(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\mathbb{P}\left(v^{T}(\nu-(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\mu-(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\nu)\geq t\right).

Thus, we require for each
t\geq 0
and any
v\in S^{n-1}
that

\displaystyle(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\mathbb{P}\left(v^{T}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})+(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})v^{T}(\mu-\nu)\geq t\right)+(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\mathbb{P}\left(v^{T}(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})(\nu-\mu)\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}}),

for some
\sigma^{\prime}
of the same order as
\sigma
(which is equivalent to the moment generating definition of a sub-Gaussian random variable by
Vershynin [2018, Proposition 2.5.2]
). By a standard Gaussian tail bound (see Lemma
B.4
), the left-hand side is smaller than

\displaystyle\frac{1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{(t-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}v^{T}(\mu-\nu))^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)+(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\mathbb{P}\left(v^{T}(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})(\nu-\mu)\geq t\right).

(A.2)

It thus suffices to bound (
A.2
) with
\exp(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}})
.

We will now require that
\|\mu-\nu\|\asymp\sigma\sqrt{\log 1/\epsilon}\wedge\tfrac{d}{\epsilon}
(which is possible due to Lemma
1.3
) to simultaneously ensure the mixture is sub-Gaussian and also sufficiently separated. Now consider two cases:

Case 1:

v^{T}(\mu-\nu)\geq 0
. Then the second term in (
A.2
) is
0
and the first term is
\leq\frac{1-\epsilon/2}{2}
. For
t\leq\sqrt{2}\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{\log\tfrac{2}{1-\epsilon/2}}
, the desired bound on (
A.2
) by
\exp(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}})
will automatically hold. On the other hand, suppose
t\geq\sqrt{2}\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{\log\tfrac{2}{1-\epsilon/2}}
. Noting that
\sqrt{\log\tfrac{2}{1-\epsilon/2}}\geq\sqrt{\log 2}\geq\epsilon\sqrt{\log 1/\epsilon}
, we have
t\geq\sqrt{2}\sigma^{\prime}(\epsilon\sqrt{\log 1/\epsilon}\wedge d)\gtrsim\epsilon\|\mu-\nu\|
. Thus, in the first term of (
A.2
), we have

\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}v^{T}(\mu-\nu)\leq\epsilon\|\mu-\nu\|\lesssim t

and hence we can fold
\epsilon v^{T}(\mu-\nu)
into
t
. So our desired bound will hold for
\sigma^{\prime}
slightly bigger than
\sigma
.

Case 2:
Suppose now
v^{T}(\mu-\nu)\leq 0
. Then the first term in (
A.2
) is smaller than
\frac{1-\epsilon/2}{2}\exp(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).
The second term is smaller than
\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}
for
t\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\log 1/\epsilon}
and is
0
otherwise. Hence it suffices to argue that

\displaystyle\tfrac{1-\epsilon/2}{2}\exp(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})+\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}})

for
t\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\log 1/\epsilon}
(since otherwise the sum is clearly
\leq\exp(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}})
). Well, since
\sigma^{\prime}>\sigma
this is implied if
\epsilon\leq(1+\epsilon/2)\exp(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2{\sigma^{\prime}}^{2}})
. But this holds for
t\leq\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{\log 1/\epsilon}
. This completes the proof that the mixture is sub-Gaussian for both cases.

Having verified this is a sub-Gaussian setting, we now pick a corruption procedure
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
that lets us lower bound the minimax rate. First, fix an estimator
\hat{\mu}
. Now observe that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu\in K}\sup_{\xi}\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|\hat{\mu}(\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X}))-\mu\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X})-\mu\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\tfrac{1}{2}\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X}))-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\|^{2},

(A.3)

since the worst case risk across all
\mu\in K
and sub-Gaussian distributions
\xi
is larger than the average of the risks when
\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}
and when
\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes N}
.

Next, our adversary, given knowledge of which scenario is presented, will do nothing in the fully Gaussian setting, where
\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}
. On the other hand, in the mixture setting
\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes N}
, the adversary does the following. Let
W=\sum_{i}W_{i}
count the number of
\nu
that are drawn, in place of a Gaussian. The adversary, given knowledge of the chosen distribution, observes
W
and if
W\leq\epsilon N
, substitutes each observed
X_{i}=\nu
with a freshly drawn Gaussian observation. If
W>\epsilon N
, the adversary does nothing. Hence, the adversary converts an i.i.d. mixture to either i.i.d. Gaussians or leaves the data unchanged, depending on the outcome of
W\sim\operatorname{Bin}(N,\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})
. Furthermore, note that the number of outliers is bounded by
\epsilon N
so this is a valid corruption procedure
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
.

Now, by
[
Kaas and Buhrman, 1980
, Theorem 1]
, given
\epsilon<1/2
, any
k
for which
(N-1)\epsilon/2\leq k\leq(N+1)\epsilon/2
will satisfy a median property:
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Bin}(N,\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\geq k)>1/2
and
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Bin}(N,\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\leq k)>1/2
. In our setting, we take
k=\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}N
.

Thus, the risk with this corruption procedure
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
is lower than the most effective
\mathcal{C}
, so applying
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
by replacing the mixture with Gaussian draws when
W\leq k
(and discarding the portion of the risk when
W>k
), we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X}))-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}))-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(W\leq k)\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle>\tfrac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\|^{2}.

Note also that for the already Gaussian scenario, by comparing to a scenario with no corruption, we have

\tfrac{1}{2}\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X})-\mu\|^{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu\|^{2}.

Returning to (
A.3
), the quantity
\sup_{\mu\in K}\sup_{\xi}\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|\hat{\mu}(\mathcal{C}(\tilde{X}))-\mu\|^{2}
is thus lower bounded by

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu\|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}}\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{8}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})^{\otimes N}}\left(\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu\|+\|\hat{\mu}(\tilde{X})-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\|\mu-\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}\|^{2}\asymp\epsilon^{2}\|\mu-\nu\|^{2}\asymp\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\wedge d^{2},

recalling our earlier requirement on
\mu
and
\nu
. The second line follows from
(x+y)^{2}\leq 2(x^{2}+y^{2})
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Taking the infimum over all estimators, we obtain the result.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
3

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

First, note that at level
1
, we have both a minimal
d
-covering and maximal
d
-packing of
K
by our root node
\bar{\nu}
, and at level
2
, we have a maximal
d/c
-packing of
B(\bar{\nu},d)\cap K=K
and hence a
d/c
-covering of
K
. Moreover, there is no pruning at these steps.

Let us now prove our claim about a
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}c}
-covering of
K
for
J\geq 3
. We proceed by induction. We start with our base case
J=3
. Pick any point
x\in K
. Because at
J=2
we had a
d/c
-covering of
K
, there exists a node
u\in\mathcal{L}(2)
such that
\|x-u\|\leq d/c
. Recall to form the next level, we first created a maximal
\tfrac{d}{4c}
-packing set of
B(u,d/2)\cap K
. Since
d/c<d/2
, it follows
x\in B(u,d/2)\cap K
. Hence for some
u^{\prime}\in B(u,d/2)\cap K
that either belongs to
\mathcal{L}(3)
or was pruned from
\mathcal{L}(3)
, we have
\|x-u^{\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{4c}
. If
u^{\prime}
was pruned, then there exists some
u^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(3)
so that
\|u-u^{\prime\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{4c}
, which by the triangle inequality implies
\|x-u^{\prime\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2c}
. In either case, there exists a node in
\mathcal{L}(3)
within distance
\tfrac{d}{2c}
to
x
, proving the covering claim for
J=3
.

Suppose we have shown for some
J\geq 3
that
\mathcal{L}(J)
forms a
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}c}
-covering of
K
. Pick any
x\in K
. Then for some
u\in\mathcal{L}(J)
,
\|x-u\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}c}
. Our algorithm then constructs a maximal
\tfrac{d}{2^{J}c}
-packing of
B(u,\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}})\cap K
. Since
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}c}<\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}}
, it follows
x\in B(u,\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}})\cap K
. Then for some
u^{\prime}\in B(u,\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}})\cap K
that either belongs to
\mathcal{L}(J+1)
or was pruned from
\mathcal{L}(J+1)
,
\|x-u^{\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J}c}
since we make a maximal packing. If
u^{\prime}
was pruned, there is some
u^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(J+1)
such that
\|u^{\prime}-u^{\prime\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J}c}
, which implies by the triangle inequality that
\|x-u^{\prime\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
. In either case, there exists a point in
\mathcal{L}(J+1)
within distance
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
of
x
. This completes the induction.

Now let us verify the packing claim. Suppose at any
J\geq 3
we have distinct points
u,u^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{L}(J)
such that
\|u-u^{\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
. By definition of
\mathcal{L}(J)
, neither
u
nor
u^{\prime}
was ever pruned, hence we never constructed a set
\mathcal{T}_{J}(u^{\prime\prime})
that contained
u
or
u^{\prime}
since otherwise we would have removed those points. Suppose without loss of generality that
u
is less than
u^{\prime}
lexicographically. This means at some point in our pruning algorithm,
u
was the first element of our list of unprocessed nodes
\mathcal{U}_{J}
. But because
\|u-u^{\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
and
u^{\prime}
appears after
u
in
\mathcal{U}_{J}
, this by definition implies
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{J}(u)
, contradicting the fact
u^{\prime}
was not pruned. Thus, for any points
u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(J)
,
\|u-u^{\prime}\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
, verifying that we have a
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
-packing as claimed for all
J\geq 3
.

Now we prove the covering property of
\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{J-1})
for
J\geq 2
. The
J=2
case follows from the base case remarks earlier. Assume
J\geq 3
, consider a parent node
\Upsilon_{J-1}\in\mathcal{L}(J-1)
, and let
\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{J-1})
be its offspring. Recall that pre-pruning we construct a maximal
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
-packing of
B(\Upsilon_{J-1},\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}})\cap K
, which means it is also a
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
-covering. Pick any
x\in B(\Upsilon_{J-1},\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}})\cap K
and let us show there exists an element of
\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{J-1})
within distance
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
of
x
. Well for some
u
in this maximal packing, we have
\|x-u\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
. If
u
does not get pruned, then
u\in\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{J-1})
and the claim is proven. Suppose
u
is pruned. That means for some other node
u^{\prime}
whose parent set
\mathcal{P}(u^{\prime})
initially does not contain
\Upsilon_{J-1}
, we have
\|u^{\prime}-u\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
. But recall that we then draw a directed edge from
\Upsilon_{J-1}
to
u^{\prime}
, so
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{J-1})
. By the triangle inequality, we have
\|x-u^{\prime}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}c}
. In either case, we have a
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}c}
-covering of
B(\Upsilon_{J-1},\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}})\cap K
by elements of
\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{J-1})
.

To see the final implication of the lemma suppose first that
J\geq 3
. Note that the cardinality of
\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{J-1})
is upper bounded by the cardinality of the original maximal packing set of
B(\Upsilon_{J-1},\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}})\cap K
at a distance
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}c}
, which completes the proof upon invoking the definition of local entropy. For
J=2
the cardinality is upper bounded by
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d,c)\leq\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d,2c)
as claimed.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

From Lemma
3.1
, we know for
J\geq 4
that
\mathcal{L}(J-1)
forms a
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}c}
-packing of
K
. Taking the intersection with
B(\mu,\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}})
, we obtain a
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}c}
-packing of
B(\mu,\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}})\cap K
. By definition, this quantity is bounded by
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d}{2^{J-2}},c)
.

For
J=2
, note there is only a single element in
\mathcal{L}(1)
, so the cardinality is bounded by 1 and thus
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d,c)
. For
J=3
, recall that
\mathcal{L}(2)
was formed by a
d/c
-covering of
B(\bar{\nu},d)\cap K
where
\bar{\nu}
is our root node, and this is bounded by definition by
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d,c)\leq\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d/2,c)
(where the last inequality follows from Lemma
1.4
) which is in turn bounded by
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d/2,2c)
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

Recall
\Upsilon_{1}
is just our root node, and
\Upsilon_{2}
is chosen from a
d/c
-packing of
B(\Upsilon_{1},d)\cap K
(and we need not prune). Then for each
k\geq 3
, observe that
\Upsilon_{k}
is chosen by first taking a
\tfrac{d}{2^{k-1}c}
-packing of
B(\Upsilon_{k-1},\tfrac{d}{2^{k-2}})\cap K
and then possibly pruning the tree. If
\Upsilon_{k}
was pruned, that means for some
u
in this
\tfrac{d}{2^{k-1}c}
-packing of
B(\Upsilon_{k-1},\tfrac{d}{2^{k-2}})\cap K
, we have
\|\Upsilon_{k}-u\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{k-1}c}
. By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|\Upsilon_{k}-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\|\Upsilon_{k}-u\|+\|u-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{k-1}c}+\tfrac{d}{2^{k-2}}.

If
\Upsilon_{k}
was not pruned,
\|\Upsilon_{k}-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{k-2}}
. In summary,
\|\Upsilon_{2}-\Upsilon_{1}\|\leq d
, and for any
k\geq 3
,
\|\Upsilon_{k}-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{k-1}c}+\tfrac{d}{2^{k-2}}
.

If
J\geq J^{\prime}\geq 2
, then we have

\displaystyle\|\Upsilon_{J}-\Upsilon_{J^{\prime}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=J^{\prime}+1}^{J}\|\Upsilon_{k}-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\sum_{k=J^{\prime}+1}^{J}\left[\tfrac{d}{2^{k-1}c}+\tfrac{d}{2^{k-2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2d}{c}\left[\tfrac{1}{2^{J^{\prime}}}-\tfrac{1}{2^{J}}\right]+4d\left[\tfrac{1}{2^{J^{\prime}}}-\tfrac{1}{2^{J}}\right]\leq\left(\tfrac{2d}{c}+4d\right)\cdot\tfrac{1}{2^{J^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d(2+4c)}{c2^{J^{\prime}}}.

If
J^{\prime}=1
, then clearly
\|\Upsilon_{J}-\Upsilon_{1}\|\leq d
since both points belong to
K
, but
d\leq\frac{d(2+4c)}{c\cdot 2}
clearly holds. Therefore, we conclude for all
J\geq J^{\prime}\geq 1
, we have
\|\Upsilon_{J}-\Upsilon_{J^{\prime}}\|\leq\frac{d(2+4c)}{c2^{J^{\prime}}}
.
∎

Each setting of the paper requires checking the existence of different absolute constants that appear in the main theorems, namely, Lemma
B.2
, Lemma
C.2
, and Lemma
C.3
. It turns out to repeatedly involve properties of the functions defined in the following lemma.

Lemma B.1
.

Define the functions
g\colon[0,1/2)\to\mathbb{R}
and
h\colon[0,1/2)\to\mathbb{R}
by

g(t)=(\tfrac{1}{2}+t)\log(\tfrac{1}{2}+t)+(\tfrac{1}{2}-t)\log(1-2t)

and
h(t)=t\left(1-\exp(\tfrac{2g(t)}{1/2-t})\right)
. Then the following properties hold:

(i)

g,h
are both continuous

(ii)

g(t)<0
for
t\in[0,1/2)

(iii)

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\tfrac{-2g(t)}{1/2-t}=\log 4

(iv)

\lim_{t\uparrow 1/2}\tfrac{-2g(t)}{1/2-t}=\infty

(v)

\tfrac{-2g(t)}{1/2-t}
is increasing on
(0,1/2)

(vi)

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}h(t)=0

(vii)

\lim_{t\uparrow 1/2}h(t)=1/2

(viii)

h(t)/t\in(0,1)
for
t\in(0,1/2)

(ix)

h
is a surjection onto
(0,1/2)

Proof.

Property (
i
) is evident from standard logarithm and exponential properties. (
ii
) follows by separately checking the sign of each term of
g
. Substitute
t=0
and apply (
i
) to obtain (
iii
). To verify (
iv
), expand the definition of
g
and one obtains a sum of terms, the first tending to
0
, the second tending to
\infty
. To obtain (
v
), differentiate to obtain
-8\log(t+1/2)/(1-2t)^{2}
and this is positive on
(0,1/2)
.
(
vi
) and (
vii
) then follow by applying (
iii
) and (
iv
). (
viii
) holds since the input to the exponential is negative. (
ix
) follows from (
i
), (
vi
), (
vii
), and the intermediate value theorem.
∎

Now we proceed to proving our Gaussian Type I error bound in Theorem
3.6
. We first prove a purely technical result which establishes that the absolute constants we use actually exist. We then state two tail bounds on Gaussian random variables that we use conditional on the size of
\delta/\sigma
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,1/2]
and
C>2
be fixed constants. Denote
C^{\prime}=\frac{C-2}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}>0
and let
g
be as defined in Lemma
B.1
. Then there exist constants
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, and
L>0
depending on
C
and
\kappa
only with the following properties. Assume
\epsilon\leq 1/2-\kappa
. Let
\delta>0
and
\sigma>0
be given. Denote
\varrho=\exp(-\tfrac{{C^{\prime}}^{2}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})
. Now assume
C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma>L
. Then

(i)

\epsilon<\alpha(1-\varrho)

(ii)

(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho)\geq-2g(\alpha)
.

Proof.

First, select
\alpha\in[0,1/2)
such that

1/2-\kappa<\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)\right).

(B.1)

Such an
\alpha
exists since the right-hand side is a surjection onto
(0,1/2)
as proven in (
ix
) of Lemma
B.1
.

Next, pick
\beta>0
such that

\Phi^{-1}(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{\beta}{C^{\prime}})>\sqrt{-\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{(1/2-\alpha)}}.

(B.2)

Note that the term inside the square root is strictly positive since
g(\alpha)<0
for any
\alpha\in[0,1/2)
, using (
ii
) of Lemma
B.1
.

Then, we set

C_{1}=2\Phi^{-1}(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{\beta}{C^{\prime}})/\beta.

(B.3)

Now set
L=\beta C_{1}/2
and

C_{2}=\tfrac{C^{\prime}(\Phi(L)-1/2)}{L},

(B.4)

which is positive since
\Phi(L)>1/2
when
L>0
. Note that (
B.3
) rearranges to
\Phi(\beta C_{1}/2)-1/2=\beta/C^{\prime}
so that

C_{1}C_{2}=\underbrace{\tfrac{2L}{\beta}}_{=C_{1}}\cdot\underbrace{\tfrac{C^{\prime}(\Phi(L)-1/2)}{L}}_{=C_{2}}=2\cdot\tfrac{C^{\prime}}{\beta}\cdot\underbrace{(\Phi(\beta C_{1}/2)-1/2)}_{=\beta/C^{\prime}}=2.

We will use this calculation later in the paper.

Now assume
C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma>L
. This implies

1-\varrho>1-\exp(-L^{2})=1-\exp(-\beta^{2}C_{1}^{2}/4).

(B.5)

Moreover, (
B.2
) and (
B.3
) ensure

\beta C_{1}=2\Phi^{-1}(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{\beta}{C^{\prime}})>2\sqrt{-\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{(1/2-\alpha)}}.

(B.6)

Combining (
B.5
) and (
B.6
) yields
1-\varrho>1-\exp\left(\frac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)
. Then using (
B.1
) we have
1/2-\kappa<\alpha(1-\varrho)
, so assuming
\epsilon\leq 1/2-\kappa
ensures (i) holds. Finally,
1-\varrho\geq 1-\exp\left(\frac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)
rearranges to yield (ii).
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Fix a constant
L>0
. Then for all
z\in[0,L]
, we have

1-\Phi(z)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{z}{L}\cdot\left(\Phi(L)-\tfrac{1}{2}\right).

Proof.

Observe that the function
z\mapsto 1-\Phi(z)
is convex for
z\geq 0
, as its second derivative is
(-\phi(z))^{\prime}=z\phi(z)>0
for
z>0
, where
\phi:=\Phi^{\prime}
. Thus, for any value of
z\in[0,L]
we have

1-\Phi(z)\leq\left(1-\tfrac{z}{L}\right)\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{z}{L}\cdot(1-\Phi(L))=\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{z}{L}\cdot\left(\Phi(L)-\tfrac{1}{2}\right).

∎

Lemma B.4

(
[
Wozencraft and Jacobs, 1965
, equation (2.122)]
)
.

For
z\geq 0
,

1-\Phi(z)\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp(-z^{2}/2).

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

We first bound
\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)
, and at the end, argue that the full claim follows by symmetry. Using Lemma
B.2
, pick constants
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, and
L>0
depending on
C
and
\kappa
only, such that conditions (i) and (ii) hold when
C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma>L
. Recall from the lemma that we defined
C^{\prime}=(C-2)/(2\sqrt{2\pi})>0
and
\varrho=\exp\left(-\frac{{C^{\prime}}^{2}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\in(0,1)
. Also, we introduced constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
in the proof of that lemma, specifically (
B.3
) and (
B.4
).

We introduce some additional shorthand notation. Let
A_{i}
be the event that
\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\nu_{1}\|\geq\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\nu_{2}\|
, and let
B_{i}
be the event that the possibly corrupted data
X_{i}
satisfies
\|X_{i}-\nu_{1}\|\geq\|X_{i}-\nu_{2}\|
. In our notation
\psi=1
is the event that at least
N/2
of
B_{1},\dots,B_{N}
occur. We define another indicator random variable
\tilde{\psi}
in two different ways: when
C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma\leq L
we let
\tilde{\psi}=1
be the event that at least
N/2-C_{2}N\delta/(2\sigma)
of
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}
occur, and when
C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma>L
, we let
\tilde{\psi}=1
be the event at least
N/2-N\alpha(1-\varrho)
of
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}
occur.

For both of these cases, we use the following strategy: For each
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta
, we will upper bound
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i})
and as a consequence
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
using binomial concentration results. It will turn out that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
, so we will have bounded the desired quantity.

The proof of
Neykov [2022, Lemma II.5]
demonstrated that the original data
\tilde{X}_{i}
satisfies

\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i})\leq\mathbb{P}(N(m,\tau^{2})\geq 0)=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{N}(0,1)\geq-m/\tau),

where
m=(-1+2/C)\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|^{2}<0
and
\tau^{2}=4\sigma^{2}\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|^{2}
. But
|m/\tau|\geq C^{\prime}\sqrt{2\pi}\delta/\sigma
, hence we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i})\leq 1-\Phi\left(\tfrac{C^{\prime}\sqrt{2\pi}\delta}{\sigma}\right)\leq 1-\Phi\left(\tfrac{C^{\prime}\sqrt{2}\delta}{\sigma}\right)\leq 1-\Phi\left(\tfrac{C^{\prime}\delta}{\sigma}\right).

(B.7)

Fix any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta
, and let us now consider two cases. First, assume
C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma\leq L
, where
L
is the fixed constant from the previous lemma. Then apply our bound on
1-\Phi(z)
from Lemma
B.3
with
z=C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma
and the right-most bound in (
B.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i})

\displaystyle\leq 1-\Phi\left(\tfrac{C^{\prime}\delta}{\sigma}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{C^{\prime}\delta}{\sigma}\cdot\tfrac{\Phi(L)-\tfrac{1}{2}}{L}=\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{C_{2}\delta}{\sigma}.

(B.8)

In the last step we substituted
C_{2}
from (
B.4
) in Lemma
B.2
.

Let us show
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
. It suffices to show the event
\tilde{\psi}=0
is a subset of the event
\psi=0
. Suppose
\tilde{\psi}=0
, i.e., no more than
N/2-C_{2}N\delta/(2\sigma)
of
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}
occur. Now we corrupt at most
N\epsilon
of the
\tilde{X}_{i}
. Observe that if
\tilde{X}_{i}
is uncorrupted, then the event
A_{i}
occurs if and only if
B_{i}
occurs. It is only possible that
A_{i}
occurs but not
B_{i}
or vice versa if the
i
th datapoint is corrupted. In the worst case, no more than

N/2-C_{2}N\delta/(2\sigma)+N\epsilon\leq N/2+N\epsilon(1-C_{1}C_{2}/2)=N/2

of
B_{1},\dots,B_{N}
occur, where we used the assumption
\delta/\sigma\geq C_{1}\epsilon
and that
C_{1}C_{2}=2
as computed in Lemma
B.2
. This implies
\psi=0.
Thus,
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
.

Let us now bound
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
using our bound on
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}).
The event
\tilde{\psi}=1
corresponds to no more than
N/2+C_{2}\delta N/2\sigma
of
A_{1}^{c},\dots,A_{N}^{c}
being true. The probability of this event is bounded by the tail probability of a binomial random variable, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c}))\leq N/2+C_{2}\delta N/(2\sigma))
. Set
p=1/2+C_{2}\delta/\sigma
and
\zeta=C_{2}\delta/(2\sigma)
so that
p-\zeta=1/2+C_{2}\delta/(2\sigma)
. Then note that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c})\geq p
using (
B.8
).
Using the Hoeffding bound, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c}))\leq\overbrace{N/2+C_{2}\delta N/(2\sigma)}^{=N(p-\zeta)})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c}))\leq N(\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c})-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-2N\zeta^{2})

\displaystyle=\exp(-\tfrac{C_{2}^{2}N\delta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

Taking the supremum over
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta
finishes the claim in this first case.

For our second case, suppose
C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma>L
. Then we have by the penultimate bound in (
B.7
) and the normal tail bound in Lemma
B.4
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i})\leq 1-\Phi\left(\tfrac{C^{\prime}\sqrt{2}\delta}{\sigma}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\tfrac{{C^{\prime}}^{2}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)=\tfrac{\varrho}{2}.

Recall for this second case that
\tilde{\psi}=1
if at least
N/2-N\alpha(1-\varrho)
of the
A_{i}
occur, where
\alpha,\varrho
are from Lemma
B.2
. Let us show
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
by again showing the event
\tilde{\psi}=0
is a subset of
\psi=0
. Suppose no more than
N/2-N\alpha(1-\varrho)
of the
A_{i}
occur, i.e.,
\tilde{\psi}=0
. By the same logic as before, in the worst case, no more than

N/2-N\alpha(1-\varrho)+N\epsilon=N/2+N(\epsilon-\alpha(1-\varrho))\leq N/2

of the
B_{i}
occur, where we used
\epsilon<\alpha(1-\varrho)
from (i) in Lemma
B.2
. Thus
\psi=0
. Hence
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
.

Let us now bound
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
. This is bounded by
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c}))\leq N/2+N\alpha(1-\varrho))
. Now set
p=1-\varrho/2
and
\zeta=(1/2-\alpha)(1-\varrho)
. Then
p-\zeta=\frac{1}{2}+\alpha-\alpha\varrho
, and also note
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c})\geq p
. Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c}))\leq N/2+N\alpha(1-\varrho))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c}))\leq N(p-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,p)\leq N(p-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq e^{-N\cdot D((p-\zeta)\|p)},

(B.9)

where in the next to last inequality we used that
\mathrm{Bin}(N,p)\leq_{\operatorname{st}}\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c}))
5
5

5

Here we use
\leq_{\operatorname{st}}
to denote (first order) stochastic dominance, which means that
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,p)\geq x)\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c}))\geq x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
since they use the same sample size and
p\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i}^{c})

[
Klenke and Mattner, 2010
, Theorem 1(a)]
, and in the last inequality we used a Chernoff bound for the binomial distribution
[see e.g.,
Dubhashi and Panconesi, 2009
, Section 1.3]
where we define
D(q\|p)=q\log\tfrac{q}{p}+(1-q)\log\tfrac{1-q}{1-p}
. Let us now lower bound
D(p-\zeta\|p)
. We have

\displaystyle D(p-\zeta\|p)

\displaystyle=(1/2+\alpha-\alpha\varrho)\overbrace{\log\textstyle\frac{1/2+\alpha-\alpha\varrho}{1-\varrho/2}}^{\leq 0}+(1/2-\alpha+\alpha\varrho)\overbrace{\log\textstyle\frac{1/2-\alpha+\alpha\varrho}{\varrho/2}}^{\geq 0}

\displaystyle\geq(1/2+\alpha)\log\textstyle\frac{1/2+\alpha-\alpha\varrho}{1-\varrho/2}+(1/2-\alpha)\log\textstyle\frac{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha+\alpha\varrho}{\varrho/2}

\displaystyle\geq(1/2+\alpha)\log(1/2+\alpha)+(1/2-\alpha)\log\textstyle\frac{1/2-\alpha+\alpha\varrho}{\varrho/2}

\displaystyle\geq(1/2+\alpha)\log(1/2+\alpha)+(1/2-\alpha)\log\textstyle\frac{1/2-\alpha}{\varrho/2}

\displaystyle=g(\alpha)+(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho).

The first line is from substituting
p
and
\zeta
into
D(p-\zeta\|p)
. The second uses the respective sign on the logarithmic terms and sets
\varrho=0
in the terms multiplied to the logarithm. The third follows by setting
\varrho=0
in the first logarithm term by noting
\frac{d}{d\varrho}\frac{\frac{1}{2}+\alpha-\alpha\varrho}{1-\varrho/2}=\frac{1-2\alpha}{(2-\varrho)^{2}}>0
so the entire logarithmic term is increasing in
\varrho
. The fourth line uses monotonicity of the logarithm and sets
\varrho=0
in the numerator of the argument to the second logarithm term. Then we split up the logarithm terms to isolate the portion depending on
\varrho
, recalling the definition of
g(\alpha)
from Lemma
B.1
.

Now recall since
C^{\prime}\delta/\sigma>L
, we have from (ii) of Lemma
B.2
that
(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho)\geq-2g(\alpha)>0
. Returning to
D(p-\zeta\|p)
, we have

\displaystyle D(p-\zeta\|p)

\displaystyle\geq g(\alpha)+(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho)

\displaystyle\geq(1/2)(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho).

To complete the argument, note that
\log(1/\varrho)={C^{\prime}}^{2}\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
and use (
B.9
).

Having bounded
\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)
, we claim the same bound for
\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{2}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=0)
follows by symmetry. Recall
\psi=1
precisely when at least
N/2
of
X_{i}
satisfy
\|X_{i}-\nu_{2}\|\leq\|X_{i}-\nu_{1}\|
. On the other hand,
\psi=0
is equivalent to at least
N/2
of the
X_{i}
satisfying the reverse inequality,
\|X_{i}-\nu_{1}\|<\|X_{i}-\nu_{2}\|
. We can set
A_{i}
to be the event
\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\nu_{1}\|<\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\nu_{2}\|
and
B_{i}
the event
\|X_{i}-\nu_{1}\|<\|X_{i}-\nu_{2}\|
. Our Gaussian tail bound for
\tilde{X}_{i}
adapted from
Neykov [2022]
is unaffected by strict inequalities versus weak ones. Specifically, we have the modified bound:

\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{2}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i})\leq\mathbb{P}(N(m,\tau^{2})>0)=\mathbb{P}(N(m,\tau^{2})\geq 0)=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{N}(0,1)\geq-m/\tau).

The remainder of the proof can proceed without issue.
∎

We now verify our Algorithm
2
’s error bound in Theorem
3.8
. We start with some auxiliary lemmas that handle the scenario where our
\delta/\sigma
requirements are met for the first
\tilde{J}
steps. We will require some set intersection properties—one of which we use here and the other later in the unbounded case.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
J\geq 2
be an integer and let
A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{J}
be a sequence of events. Then

(i)

\mathbb{P}(A_{J})\leq\mathbb{P}(A_{1})+\mathbb{P}(A_{1}^{c}\cap A_{2})+\sum_{j=3}^{J}\mathbb{P}(A_{1}^{c}\cap A_{j-1}^{c}\cap A_{j})
,

(ii)

\mathbb{P}(A_{J})\leq\mathbb{P}(A_{1})+\sum_{j=2}^{J}\mathbb{P}(A_{j}\cap A_{j-1}^{c})
.

Proof.

Write
AB
to denote the intersection of sets
A
and
B
. Observe

\displaystyle A_{J}

\displaystyle\subseteq A_{1}\cup(A_{1}^{c}A_{2})\cup(A_{1}^{c}A_{2}^{c}A_{3})\cup(A_{1}^{c}A_{2}^{c}A_{3}^{c}A_{4})\cup\ldots\cup(A_{1}^{c}A_{2}^{c}\ldots A_{J-1}^{c}A_{J})

\displaystyle=A_{1}\cup(A_{1}^{c}A_{2})\cup\bigcup_{j=3}^{J}\left[A_{1}^{c}\dots A_{j-1}^{c}A_{j}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq A_{1}\cup(A_{1}^{c}A_{2})\cup\bigcup_{j=3}^{J}\left[A_{1}^{c}A_{j-1}^{c}A_{j}\right]

(B.10)

\displaystyle\subseteq A_{1}\cup(A_{1}^{c}A_{2})\cup\bigcup_{j=3}^{J}\left[A_{j-1}^{c}A_{j}\right].

(B.11)

Applying the union bound to (
B.10
) yields (
i
), and similarly the union bound with (
B.11
) yields (
ii
).
∎

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\eta_{J}
be defined as in Theorem
3.8
and
C=c/2-1
. Suppose
\tilde{J}
is such that (
3.2
) holds and also
\frac{d}{2^{\tilde{J}-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
. Then for each such
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}}\right)\leq 2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

Proof.

Note that if
\tilde{J}
satisfies (
3.2
) and also
\frac{d}{2^{\tilde{J}-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
, it is clear that any
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
satisfies these conditions. Observe that for
3\leq j\leq\tilde{J}
, if
\|\Upsilon_{j-1}-\mu\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}}
, then
\Upsilon_{j-1}=u
for some
u\in\mathcal{L}(j-1)\cap B(\mu,\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}})
. Applying a union bound, setting
\delta=\frac{d}{2^{j-1}(C+1)}
, substituting the update rule for
\Upsilon_{j}
from Algorithm
2
, and finally dropping the intersection, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{j-1}},\|\Upsilon_{j-1}-\mu\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{u\in\mathcal{L}(j-1)\cap B(\mu,\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{j-1}},\Upsilon_{j-1}=u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{L}(j-1)\cap B(\mu,\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\|\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}(u)}T(\delta,\nu,\mathcal{O}(u))-\mu\big\|>(C+1)\delta,\Upsilon_{j-1}=u\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{u\in\mathcal{L}(j-1)\cap B(\mu,\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}})}\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\|\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}(u)}T(\delta,\nu,\mathcal{O}(u))-\mu\big\|>(C+1)\delta\Big).

Note
\mathcal{L}(j-1)\cap B(\mu,\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}})
has cardinality upper bounded by
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}},2c)
by Lemma
3.2
. Set
K^{\prime}=B(u,\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}})\cap K\subseteq K
, and recall from Lemma
3.1
that
\mathcal{O}(u)
forms a
\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}c}=\tfrac{d}{2^{j-1}(C+1)}=\delta
-covering of
K^{\prime}
with cardinality bounded by
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}},2c)
. Moreover,
\delta\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\sigma\epsilon
holds by assumption. Applying Lemma
3.7
and noting the summands become constant in
u
, we may bound the probability term above:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{j-1}},\|\Upsilon_{j-1}-\mu\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}})

\displaystyle\leq\Big[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d}{2^{j-2}},2c)\Big]^{2}\cdot\exp\Big(-\tfrac{C_{3}(\kappa)N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\Big(\tfrac{2(C+1)\eta_{j}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c\Big)\Big]^{2}\cdot\exp\Big(-\tfrac{N\eta_{j}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\Big).

Here we used the definition
\eta_{j}=\tfrac{d\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}{2^{j-1}(C+1)}=\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}\delta
.

Now define the event
A_{j}=\{\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{j-1}}\}
and observe for any
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
that

\mathbb{P}(A_{J})\leq\mathbb{P}(A_{1})+\mathbb{P}(A_{2}\cap A_{1}^{c})+\sum_{j=3}^{J}\mathbb{P}(A_{j}\cap A_{j-1}^{c}),

a proof of which is given in Lemma
B.5
. We have already bounded
\mathbb{P}(A_{j}\cap A_{j-1}^{c})
for
3\leq j\leq\tilde{J}
. Note that
\mathbb{P}(A_{1})=0
since
\Upsilon_{1}
and
\mu
both belong to a set of diameter
d
. Recall that for the second level, we construct a maximal
d/c
-packing of
B(\bar{\nu},d)\cap K
, which is therefore a
d/c
-covering of
B(\bar{\nu},d)\cap K
(without pruning). So we may apply Lemma
3.7
and conclude

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A_{2}\cap A_{1}^{c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(A_{2})\leq\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d,c)\cdot\exp\left(-\tfrac{C_{3}(\kappa)N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{2(C+1)\eta_{2}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c)\right]^{2}\cdot\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{2}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

Note that
\eta_{J}
is decreasing in
J
while
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}
is a non-increasing function
, so we may bound
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{2(C+1)\eta_{j}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c)
with
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{2(C+1)\eta_{J}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c)
for any
j\leq J
. Therefore for
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\tfrac{2(C+1)\eta_{J}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c\right)\right]^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{J}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{j}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\tfrac{2(C+1)\eta_{J}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c\right)\right]^{2}\frac{a_{J}}{1-a_{J}},

where we set
a_{J}=\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})
.

Now suppose that
\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}>2\log\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\tfrac{2(C+1)\eta_{J}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c\right)\right]^{2}\vee\log 2
, i.e., (
3.2
) holds. Observe that
\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}>\log 2
implies
a_{J}<1/2
. Then we conclude

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp(\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})\cdot a_{J}\cdot\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{1-a_{J}}}_{\leq 2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp(\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})\cdot\underbrace{\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})}_{=a_{J}}

\displaystyle=2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}),

giving us an upper bound on
\mathbb{P}(A_{J})
so long as (
3.2
) holds as well as our
\frac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
condition.
∎

Lemma B.7
.

Let
\eta_{J}
be defined as in Theorem
3.8
. Suppose
\tilde{J}
is such (
3.2
) holds and also
\frac{d}{2^{\tilde{J}-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
. Let
C_{4}(\kappa)=\tfrac{(19+16C)^{2}}{4C_{3}(\kappa)}
. Then if
\nu^{\ast\ast}
denotes the output after at least
J^{\ast}
iterations, we have

\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\leq C_{4}(\kappa)\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4C_{4}(\kappa)\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\Big(-\tfrac{N\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Big).

Proof.

By Lemma
B.6
, we have for
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
that

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}}\right)\leq 2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}),

i.e., upper bounding
\mathbb{P}(A_{j})
where
A_{j}=\{\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d}{2^{j-1}}\}
.

Now recall our definition of
J^{\ast}
and let
\nu^{\ast}=\Upsilon_{J^{\ast}}
be the output of
J^{\ast}-1
steps. Define
B_{J}
to be the event that
\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>\omega\eta_{J}
where
\omega=\tfrac{7+6C}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}.
We now upper bound
\mathbb{P}(B_{J})
assuming (
3.2
) holds. By Lemma
3.3
, for any
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
(which implies
J\leq J^{\ast}
) we have

\|\Upsilon_{J}-\nu^{\ast}\|\leq\tfrac{d(2+4c)}{c2^{J}}=\left(\tfrac{1}{C+1}+4\right)\cdot\tfrac{d}{2^{J}}

using
c=2(C+1)
. Note that
\mathbb{P}(A_{J}^{c})\leq\mathbb{P}(B_{J}^{c})
, for if
\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}}
, then by the triangle inequality and the definition of
\omega
and
\eta_{J}
, we have

\|\nu^{\ast}-\mu\|\leq\|\nu^{\ast}-\Upsilon_{J}\|+\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|\leq\left(\tfrac{1}{C+1}+4\right)\cdot\tfrac{d}{2^{J}}+\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}}=\omega\eta_{J}.

Thus, for
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
,

\underbrace{\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>\omega\eta_{J})}_{=\mathbb{P}(B_{J})}\leq\mathbb{P}(A_{J})\leq 2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

(B.12)

But this result in fact holds for all integers
J\leq 0
. For such
J
we have
\omega\eta_{J}>\frac{d}{2^{J-2}}\geq 4d
so both sides of the inequality are 0. Moreover, note that
\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\leq\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)
for all
J\leq\tilde{J}
.

Now, since
\bigcup_{-\infty<J\leq\tilde{J}}[\eta_{J},\eta_{J-1})=[\eta_{\tilde{J}},\infty)
and
\eta_{J}=\eta_{J-1}/2
, we observe that any
x\geq\eta_{\tilde{J}}
belongs to some interval
[\eta_{J},\eta_{J-1})
for
J\leq\tilde{J}
and therefore satisfies
2\omega x\geq 2\omega\eta_{J}=\omega\eta_{J-1}
. Hence for
x\geq\eta_{\tilde{J}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>2\omega x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>\omega\eta_{J-1})

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J-1}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\exp(-\tfrac{Nx^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

(B.13)

Note the use of the monotonicity of
x\mapsto\exp(-\tfrac{Nx^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})
in the final inequality.

We are interested in performing at least
J^{\ast}
steps, so let
{\nu^{\ast\ast}}
be the output of
J^{\ast\ast}\geq J^{\ast}
steps. Then by Lemma
3.3
, for
x\geq\eta_{\tilde{J}}
(noting
\tilde{J}\leq J^{\ast}
means
\eta_{\tilde{J}}\geq\eta_{J^{\ast}}
) we have

\displaystyle\|\nu^{\ast}-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|

\displaystyle=\|\Upsilon_{J^{\ast}}-\Upsilon_{J^{\ast\ast}+1}\|\leq\tfrac{d(2+4c)}{c2^{J^{\ast}}}=\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\omega\eta_{J^{\ast}}\leq\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\omega\eta_{\tilde{J}}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\omega x.

The triangle inequality implies

\displaystyle\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\leq\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|+\|\nu^{\ast}-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\leq\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|+\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\omega x.

(B.14)

Set
\omega^{\prime}=(2+\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C})\omega
, which after substitution with
\omega=\tfrac{7+6C}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}
becomes
\omega^{\prime}=\tfrac{19+16C}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}
. Then for
x\geq\eta_{\tilde{J}}
, using (
B.13
) and (
B.14
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|>\omega^{\prime}x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|+\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\omega x>\omega^{\prime}x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>2\omega x)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\exp(-\tfrac{Nx^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

(B.15)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}>x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2{\omega^{\prime 2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}u\cdot\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|>\omega^{\prime}u)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq 2{\omega^{\prime 2}}\int_{0}^{\eta_{\tilde{J}}}u\mathrm{d}u+2{\omega^{\prime 2}}\int_{\eta_{\tilde{J}}}^{\infty}u\cdot\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|>\omega^{\prime}u)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq{\omega^{\prime}}^{2}\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4{\omega^{\prime}}^{2}\int_{\eta_{\tilde{J}}}^{\infty}u\exp(-\tfrac{Nu^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle={\omega^{\prime}}^{2}\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4{\omega^{\prime}}^{2}\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(19+16C)^{2}}{4C_{3}(\kappa)}\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot\tfrac{(19+16C)^{2}}{C_{3}(\kappa)}\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

∎

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

If
J^{\ast}=1
, then
\eta_{J^{\ast}}\asymp d
. Then observe that
\max(d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2})\wedge d^{2}=d^{2}
by considering the cases whether
d\leq\epsilon\sigma
or
d>\epsilon\sigma
. Since
\nu^{\ast},\mu\in K
, clearly
\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\nu^{\ast}-\mu\|^{2}\leq d^{2}
. Thus, we assume
J^{\ast}>1
.

Recall we set our initial input
\Upsilon_{1}
, and call
\Upsilon_{k+1}
the output of
k
iterations of the algorithm. Note that
J^{\ast}<\infty
since the left-hand side of (
3.2
) is an increasing function of
\eta_{J}
(thus decreasing with
J
) while the right-hand side is a non-increasing function of
\eta_{J}
(thus non-decreasing with
J
).

We now consider two cases, based on whether the
\frac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
condition fails prior to
J^{\ast}
. If it does fail, we further consider two sub-cases, depending on whether
\epsilon\gtreqqless\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
.

Case 1:
Assume
\frac{d}{2^{J^{\ast}-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
. By Lemma
B.7
with
\tilde{J}=J^{\ast}
,

\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\leq C_{4}(\kappa)\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4C_{4}(\kappa)\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

(B.16)

Note that we have
N\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}>\log 2
by definition of
J^{\ast}
, which implies
\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}<\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}/\log 2
. The exponential term is clearly bounded by
1
. Thus, we bound (
B.16
) with

C_{4}(\kappa)\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}+4C_{4}(\kappa)\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\leq C_{4}(\kappa)\left(1+\tfrac{4}{\log 2}\right)\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}.

But clearly
\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}\lesssim\max(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2})
and we know the
d^{2}
is always an upper bound, proving our claimed bound.

Case 2:
Assume for some
J^{\prime}\in\{1,2,\dots,J^{\ast}\}
we have

\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime}-1}(C+1)}<C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma.

(B.17)

If
J^{\prime}=1
, then this means
d\lesssim\epsilon\sigma
, so that our claimed upper bound reduces to just
d^{2}
and trivially holds. Suppose
J^{\prime}>1
and assuming
J^{\prime}
is chosen minimally, we know for all
1\leq J\leq J^{\prime}-1
that
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
. Then by Lemma
B.7
with
\tilde{J}=J^{\prime}-1
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\leq C_{4}(\kappa)\eta_{J^{\prime}-1}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4C_{4}(\kappa)\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J^{\prime}-1}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

(B.18)

Case 2(a):
Suppose
\epsilon\leq\tfrac{C_{5}(\kappa)}{\sqrt{N}}
where
0<C_{5}(\kappa)<\tfrac{\sqrt{\log 2}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}C_{1}(\kappa)}
. Then
\epsilon\sigma\leq C_{5}(\kappa)\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
. By definition of
J^{\prime}
in (
B.17
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{2^{J^{\prime}-1}}<C_{1}(\kappa)(C+1)\epsilon\sigma\leq C_{5}(\kappa)C_{1}(\kappa)(C+1)\cdot\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}.

Rearranging and recalling the definition of
\eta_{J}
, we have

\tfrac{N\eta_{J^{\prime}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\leq\left[\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}C_{5}(\kappa)C_{1}(\kappa)\right]^{2}<\log 2.

Thus, the condition in (
3.2
) does not hold, and by maximality of
J^{\ast}
, we must have
J^{\prime}>J^{\ast}
, which means
J^{\prime}=J^{\ast}
. But if (
3.2
) does not hold for
J^{\ast}
, we must have
J^{\ast}=1
, in which case the theorem trivially holds.

Case 2(b):
Suppose
\epsilon\geq\tfrac{C_{5}(\kappa)}{\sqrt{N}}
. This implies
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\geq C_{5}(\kappa)^{2}\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}
, and the exponential is always
\leq 1
. Thus, the entire second term in (
B.18
) in fact
\lesssim\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}
. Moreover, note by definition of
J^{\prime}
in (
B.17
) that

\eta_{J^{\prime}-1}=2\cdot\eta_{J^{\prime}}=\tfrac{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}{C+1}\cdot\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime}-1}}<2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma.

So our bound in (
B.18
) is of the form
\eta_{J^{\prime}-1}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}
, which is certainly less than
\max(\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2},\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2})
. Since the rate of any estimator outputting points in
K
is always bounded by
d^{2}
, the proof is complete.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

We split the proof in multiple cases depending on whether
\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
and
\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}>8\log 2
.

Before we proceed with our cases we begin with the edge case when
\eta^{*}=0
. Note that this implies
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta,c)=1
for any
\eta
sufficiently small, which implies that the set
K
consists of a single point (hence
d=0
) and our algorithm will trivially output that point achieving the minimax rate of
0
. Thus we may assume
\eta^{*}>0
. Note that if
\eta^{*}>0
this implies that
d>0
, which means we can make
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta,c)
bigger than any fixed constant by taking
c
sufficiently large. We now proceed with case work.

Case 1:

\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
and
\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}>8\log 2
.

First, let us derive a lower bound on the minimax rate. Observe (when
\eta^{*}>0
) that

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta^{\ast}/4,c)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta^{\ast}-\gamma,c)\geq\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\tfrac{N{(\eta^{\ast}-\gamma)}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\geq\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+4\log 2

\displaystyle\geq 4\cdot\tfrac{N{(\eta^{\ast}/4)}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+4\log 2\geq 4\left(\tfrac{N(\eta^{\ast}/4)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\vee\log 2\right).

(B.19)

Observe that the first inequality above holds since
\eta^{*}\neq 0
. Thus by (
B.19
),
\eta^{\ast}/4
satisfies the condition in Lemma
2.1
, so the minimax rate is lower bounded by
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}
up to constants. The minimax rate is also lower bounded by
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}
and thus
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})
since Lemma
2.2
applies in this case. Since
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})\leq\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})
, we obtain the claimed rate as a lower bound.

Now we obtain the upper bound. By Theorem
3.8
,
\max\left(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}\right)
is an upper bound on the minimax rate. If we can find a
\tilde{\eta}\asymp\eta^{\ast}
such that
{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}\gtrsim\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}
, which implies

\max\left(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}\right)\lesssim\max({\tilde{\eta}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}),

then
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})
upper bounds the minimax rate.

We start by obtaining a
\tilde{\eta}>0
that satisfies (
3.3
) (in lieu of
\eta_{J}
). Set
\beta=\min(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\tfrac{c}{2}\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{C_{3}(\kappa)}})\in(0,1/\sqrt{2}]
, and pick a constant
D>1
such that
D\beta>1
. We take
\tilde{\eta}=\sqrt{2}D\eta^{\ast}
. Then using the definition of
\eta^{\ast}
as a supremum
and the non-increasing property of
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\cdot,c)
, we have

\displaystyle N\tilde{\eta}^{2}/\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\beta^{-2}\cdot 2N(D\beta{\eta^{\ast}})^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq 4N(D\beta{\eta^{\ast}})^{2}/\sigma^{2}

(B.20)

\displaystyle>4\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(D\beta{\eta^{\ast}},c)\geq 4\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\bigg(\underbrace{\frac{Dc\sqrt{2}\eta^{\ast}}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}}_{\geq D\beta\eta^{\ast}},c\bigg)

\displaystyle=4\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\frac{c\tilde{\eta}}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},c\right)=2\log\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\frac{c\tilde{\eta}}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},c\right)\right]^{2}.

We also have by the assumption in Case 1 that

N\tilde{\eta}^{2}/\sigma^{2}=2ND^{2}{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}>16D^{2}\log 2>\log 2.

Thus
\tilde{\eta}
satisfies (
3.3
).

Define the non-decreasing map
\phi\colon(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
by

\phi(x)=Nx^{2}/\sigma^{2}-2\log\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\frac{cx}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},c\right)\right]^{2}\vee\log 2.

We have
\phi(\tilde{\eta})>0
since
\tilde{\eta}
satisfies (
3.3
).

First, suppose some
\eta_{J}
for
J\geq 1
satisfies (
3.3
). Then we know by maximality of
J^{\ast}
that
\eta_{J^{\ast}+1}=\eta_{J^{\ast}}/2
will satisfy
\phi(\eta_{J^{\ast}+1})<0<\phi(\tilde{\eta})
. Thus
\tilde{\eta}\geq\eta_{J^{\ast}+1}=\eta_{J^{\ast}}/2
since
\phi
is non-decreasing. If no such
\eta_{J}
exists, we must have
J^{\ast}=1
, and
\eta_{J^{\ast}}
satisfies
\phi(\eta_{J^{\ast}}/2)\leq\phi(\eta_{J^{\ast}})<0<\phi(\tilde{\eta})
. Again
\tilde{\eta}\geq\eta_{J^{\ast}}/2
. We have therefore found a
\tilde{\eta}\asymp\eta^{\ast}
such that
{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}\gtrsim\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}
and by our previous remarks, this completes the upper bound and thus Case 1.

Case 2:

\epsilon\leq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
and
\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}>8\log 2
.

The upper bound argument in Case 1 is completely unchanged since we did not use our stated assumption on
\epsilon
. Thus
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})
is still an upper bound on the minimax rate.

Proceeding to the lower bound, we can repeat an identical argument as in Case 1 and conclude that
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}
and therefore
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}
is a lower bound on the minimax rate. But we cannot invoke Lemma
2.2
, and must directly show
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}
(and consequently
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}
) is a lower bound on the minimax rate, in which case
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})
will also be a lower bound

To see this, note that if we take
\tilde{\eta}=\epsilon\sigma
, then
\tfrac{N{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}=N\epsilon^{2}\leq 1
since
\epsilon\leq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
while on the other hand
\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}>8\log 2
. Thus,
\epsilon\sigma=\tilde{\eta}<\eta^{\ast}
, and since
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}
is a lower bound on the minimax rate, so is
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}
. This completes Case 2.

Case 3:

\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
and
\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\leq 8\log 2
.

We first show that
d^{2}
and hence
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}
is a lower bound. By definition of
\eta^{\ast}
as a supremum, we have
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(2\eta^{\ast},c)<N(2\eta^{\ast})^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq 32\log 2
. Now we claim that it is impossible to fit a line segment of length
4\eta^{\ast}
inside
K
. Suppose not, i.e., there is some line segment
l
of this length contained in
K
. Let
B
be the ball formed by taking
l
as one of its diameters. Partition
l
into
2c
sub-intervals of length
2\eta^{\ast}/c
. By choosing
c
large enough, we can produce a
2\eta^{\ast}/c
-packing set of
K\cap B
of cardinality exceeding
\exp(32\log 2)
, violating our claim that
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(2\eta^{\ast},c)\leq 32\log 2
