. Thus, no such line segment exists. However, Lemma
1.3
implies a line segment of length
d/3
exists. So we conclude
d/3\leq 4\eta^{\ast}\leq 8\sigma\sqrt{2\log 2/N}
.

Now take
\tilde{\eta}=d/6
. Then
\tfrac{2N{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}=\tfrac{2N(d/6)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\leq 64\log 2
using our bound on
d
. On the other hand, we can show that for sufficiently large
c
,
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tilde{\eta},c)>64\log 2
. To see this, take one of the line segments in
K
of length
d/3
and again partition this diameter into sub-intervals of length
d/(6c)
. For sufficiently large
c
, we can pick
\exp(64\log 2)
points of distance
d/(6c)
-apart in the set
K
intersected with a ball of radius
d/6
centered at a point in
K
. Hence
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tilde{\eta},c)>\tfrac{2N{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\vee 4\log 2
. This means the condition in Lemma
2.1
with
\tilde{\eta}=d/3
holds, and the minimax rate is lower bounded by
d^{2}
(up to constants) and in turn
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}
.

Then since
\epsilon\geq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
, we have by Lemma
2.2
that
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}
and hence
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}
is a lower bound, and this proves the minimax lower bound of
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})
.

Proceeding to the upper bound, we know
d^{2}
is always an upper bound, and
d^{2}\leq 12^{2}\eta^{\ast 2}
. Hence
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}
is an upper bound up to constants, which implies
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})
is an upper bound as desired.

Case 4:

\epsilon\leq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
and
\tfrac{N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\leq 8\log 2
.

The proof that
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}
is a lower bound as well as that
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2})
is an upper bound is identical to Case 3, as neither of the arguments used our condition on
\epsilon
. The only remaining claim to prove is that
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}
is a lower bound.

It suffices to show that
\tilde{\eta}=\tfrac{1}{3\sqrt{2}}\cdot(\epsilon\sigma\wedge d)
satisfies the condition in Lemma
2.1
so that
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}
forms a lower bound up to constants. Well, our assumption on
\epsilon
implies
\tfrac{2N{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\leq N\epsilon^{2}/9\leq 1/9
. Similar to before, take a connected subset of a diameter of length
d/(3\sqrt{2})
and repeat the argument from the previous case to produce a
d/(3c\sqrt{2})
-packing set of cardinality at least
\exp(4\log 2)
for a sufficiently large choice of
c
. Then we have
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{1}{3\sqrt{2}}(\epsilon\sigma\wedge d),c)\geq\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d/(3\sqrt{2}),c)\geq 4\log 2
and also
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{1}{3\sqrt{2}}(\epsilon\sigma\wedge d),c)\geq 1/9\geq 2N{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}
. Thus, the condition in Lemma
2.1
applies, demonstrating that
\epsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}\wedge d^{2}
is a lower bound for sufficiently large
c
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Section
4

C.1
Proofs: Symmetric sub-Gaussian Noise

First, we develop a concentration bound using a local central limit theorem from
Petrov [1976, Chapter VII, Theorem 10]
. After another lemma specifying some appropriate constants, we then prove our main Type I error bound. Recall that
R_{i}
refers to the Gaussian noise we add to our data, and
\widetilde{X}_{i}
is our notation for the uncorrupted data.

Lemma C.1
.

There exists universal constants
D_{1},D_{2}>0
that are independent of the sub-Gaussian distribution such that for any integer
k>2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}
, if
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma<\frac{2D_{2}}{(C-2)}\cdot\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{k}{2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}}\right)}
, then

\mathbb{P}\left(0\leq\sqrt{k}(\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu)^{T}v\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}\delta(C-2)}{2}\right)\geq\frac{\delta(C-2)}{2\sigma D_{2}}.

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

Set
Y_{i}=\sigma^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\mu+R_{i})^{T}v
which is sub-Gaussian with a constant parameter. Define
B_{k}=\sum_{i\in G_{j}}\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2}]
, noting this quantity is the same for any group
G_{j}
. Using moment properties of sub-Gaussian random variables
[
Vershynin, 2018
, Proposition 2.5.2]
, we have
B_{k}\lesssim k
. But also
B_{k}\gtrsim k
since
\mathbb{E}|Y_{i}|^{2}\geq\mathbb{E}|\sigma^{-1}R_{i}^{T}v|^{2}=1
. Hence
B_{k}\asymp k
up to absolute constants that do not depend on the particular choice of sub-Gaussian distribution. Moreover, the moment properties imply
\sum_{i\in G_{j}}\mathbb{E}|Y_{i}|^{3}\lesssim k=O(B_{k})
. As a consequence, we also have that
B_{k}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
.

The characteristic function of
Y_{i}
satisfies

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}\exp(itY_{i})|

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{E}\exp(it\sigma^{-1}(\tilde{X}_{i}-\mu){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}v)|\cdot|\mathbb{E}\exp(it\sigma^{-1}R_{i}{{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}v)|\leq\exp(-t^{2}/2),

where in the last step we used that
|\mathbb{E}\exp(i\theta)|\leq 1
for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and that
\sigma^{-1}R_{i}^{T}v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Therefore,
Petrov [1976, assumption (2.6)]
is implied if

\int_{|t|\geq\gamma}\exp(-kt^{2}/2)\mathrm{d}t=O(1/k)

for every
\gamma>0
.

Now, with some rearranging and an application of Chebyshev’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{|t|\geq\gamma}\exp(-kt^{2}/2)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi k^{-1}}\int_{|t|\geq\gamma}\frac{\exp(-t^{2}/(2k^{-1}))}{\sqrt{2\pi k^{-1}}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi k^{-1}}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,k^{-1})}(|Z|>\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(\sqrt{2\pi k^{-1}})k^{-1}}{\gamma^{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\gamma^{2}k\sqrt{k}}=O(1/k).

Before we apply
Petrov [1976, Chapter VII, Theorem 10]
, observe that the theorem is stated for sufficiently large
k
. We want
k
to be a true absolute constant, i.e., independent of the choice of sub-Gaussian random variable. In the theorem’s proof, this requirement on
k
is only applied when using the implied condition that
L_{k}\sqrt{B_{k}}\lesssim 1
, where
L_{k}=B_{k}^{-3/2}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}\mathbb{E}|Y_{i}|^{3}
. From our earlier remarks, we know
B_{k}=\sum_{i\in G_{j}}\mathbb{E}|Y_{i}|^{2}\gtrsim k
and
\sum_{i\in G_{j}}\mathbb{E}|Y_{i}|^{3}\lesssim k
, both up to absolute constants independent of the distribution. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle L_{k}\sqrt{B_{k}}=\frac{\sum_{i\in G_{j}}\mathbb{E}|Y_{i}|^{3}}{\sum_{i\in G_{j}}\mathbb{E}|Y_{i}|^{2}}\lesssim 1.

Note that
B_{k}\gtrsim k
critically relied on our addition of Gaussian noise.

Having verified the assumptions of the theorem, we have that for our choice of fixed
k
the density
p_{k}
of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{B}_{k}}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}Y_{i}
exists and satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{x}\left|p_{k}(x)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-x^{2}/2)\right|=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\right).

For some
D_{1}>0
, we have
p_{k}(x)\geq-\frac{D_{1}}{\sqrt{k}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-x^{2}/2)
. Thus, we set
D^{\prime}=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{k}{2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}}\right)}
and assume
x\in[0,D^{\prime}]
to obtain

\displaystyle p_{k}(x)

\displaystyle>-\frac{D_{1}}{\sqrt{k}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-{D^{\prime}}^{2}/2)\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}.

We will assume
k>2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}
so that the expression inside the logarithm in
D^{\prime}
is larger than
1
.

Observe that there exists a distribution independent absolute constant
D_{2}>0
such that
\sqrt{B_{k}}\leq D_{2}\sqrt{k}
, which implies
D_{2}^{-1}\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{B_{k}}}
. Moreover, note that
p_{k}
is equivalently the density of

\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{B_{k}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}Y_{i}.

Therefore, returning to the lemma’s claim, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(0\leq\sqrt{k}(\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu)^{T}v\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}\delta(C-2)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(0\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}Y_{i}\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}\delta(C-2)}{2\sigma}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(0\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{B_{k}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}Y_{i}\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}\delta(C-2)}{2\sigma}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{B_{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left(0\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{B_{k}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}Y_{i}\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}\delta(C-2)}{2\sigma D_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\sqrt{k}\delta(C-2)/(2\sigma D_{2})}p_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta(C-2)}{2\sigma D_{2}}\asymp\delta/\sigma,

where the third line used
D_{2}^{-1}\leq\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{B_{k}}}
and the last line assumed that
\frac{\sqrt{k}(C-2)\delta}{2\sigma D_{2}}<D^{\prime}
(so that our
p_{k}(x)>1/\sqrt{k}
bound holds and we pull
p_{k}
out of the integrand), which is the case when
\delta/\sigma<\frac{2D_{2}}{(C-2)\sqrt{k}}\cdot\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{k}{2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}}\right)}.

∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let
C>2,\sigma>0
be given. Let the function
g(t)
be defined as in Lemma
B.1
. Then there exist absolute constants
k\in\mathbb{N},\alpha\in(0,1/2),\gamma\in(0,1)
, and
L>0
that do not depend on the distribution with the following properties. Suppose
\epsilon\in[0,\gamma/k]
. Fix
\delta>0
. Set
\beta=1-\frac{16\log 2}{L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}
and denote
\varrho=\exp\left(-\frac{\beta k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)
. Define the following conditions:

(i)

\beta\in(0,1)

(ii)

k\epsilon<\alpha(1-\varrho)

(iii)

(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho)\geq-2g(\alpha)

Then (i) always hold, and if
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma>L
, (ii) and (iii) also hold.

Proof.

Let
D_{1},D_{2}>0
be the distribution independent absolute constants from Lemma
C.1
. Take
k=\lceil 2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}\exp(\tfrac{4\log 8}{D_{2}^{2}})\vee 2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}\rceil+1
. Set
L=\frac{2D_{2}}{(C-2)}\cdot\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{k}{2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}}\right)}
, noting
k\geq 2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}
by assumption. For convenience, we use this opportunity to define
C_{1}=\tfrac{4kD_{2}}{C-2}
and
C_{2}=\frac{C-2}{2D_{2}}
, which we point out satisfy
C_{1}C_{2}=2k
for later use.

Observe that
\beta\in(0,1)
so long as
L^{2}(C-2)^{2}>16\log 2
, or equivalently, that
k>2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}\exp\left(\tfrac{4\log 2}{D_{2}^{2}}\right)
. Our choice of
k
(noting the
4\log 8
in place of
4\log 2
inside the exponential term) satisfies this, yielding (ii).

Next, observe that
1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)\in(0,1)
for
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
using (
viii
) from Lemma
B.1
, and moreover,
1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{\beta L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16}\right)\in(0,1)
since
\beta>0
from (i). Now,

1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)<1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{\beta L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16}\right)

(C.1)

holds provided
\tfrac{\beta L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16}>\tfrac{-2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}
, noting
\tfrac{-2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}>0
for
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
by (
ii
) of Lemma
B.1
. Then from (
iii
) and (
iv
) of the same lemma, we have
\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\frac{-2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}=\log 4
and
\lim_{x\to{1/2}^{-}}\frac{-2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}=\infty
. Thus, if we require
\tfrac{\beta L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16}>\log 4
(which occurs if
k>2\pi(1+D_{1})^{2}\exp\left(\tfrac{4\log 8}{D_{2}^{2}}\right)
, as we assumed), we may pick
\alpha>0
sufficiently small such that
\tfrac{\beta L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16}>\tfrac{-2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}>\log 4
. Thus, (
C.1
) holds, so we may pick a
\gamma>0
such that

\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)\right)<\gamma<\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{\beta L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16}\right)\right).

(C.2)

Now suppose
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma>L
. Then we have by the definition of
\varrho
, (
C.2
), and our assumption
\epsilon\in[0,\gamma/k]
that

\displaystyle\alpha(1-\varrho)>\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{\beta L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16}\right)\right)>\gamma\geq k\epsilon.

This yields (ii).

Lastly, observe (iii) is equivalent to
\alpha(1-\varrho)\geq\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)\right)
. Well using
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma>L
and (
C.2
), we have

\alpha(1-\varrho)\geq\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{\beta L^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16}\right)\right)\geq\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)\right).

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We will upper bound
\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)
and by symmetry we will obtain the bound for
\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{2}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=0)
by the same argument we gave at the end of the proof of Theorem
3.6
but with sub-Gaussian tail bounds instead. In particular, we note that since we add Gaussian noise, the tail bounds we use in this proof are the same with strict or weak inequality.

Using Lemma
C.2
, we pick constants
k\in\mathbb{N},\alpha\in(0,1/2),L>0,\beta\in(0,1)
such that conditions (i) from the lemma always holds and (ii) and (iii) hold when
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma>L
. Write
\varrho=\exp\left(-\frac{\beta k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)
. Recall we also defined
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
in the proof of Lemma
C.2
.

Let
A_{j}
be the event that
\big\|k^{-1}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}(\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i})-\nu_{1}\big\|\geq\big\|k^{-1}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}(\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i})-\nu_{2}\big\|
, and let
B_{j}
be the event
\big\|k^{-1}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}(X_{i}+R_{i})-\nu_{1}\big\|\geq\big\|k^{-1}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}(X_{i}+R_{i})-\nu_{2}\big\|
. Then
\psi
is an indicator variable that takes value
1
if at least
N/(2k)
of
B_{1},\dots,B_{N/k}
occur. When
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma\leq L
, let
\tilde{\psi}
to be the indicator variable that takes value
1
if at least
\tfrac{N}{2k}-\tfrac{C_{2}\delta}{\sigma}\cdot\tfrac{N}{2k}
of
A_{1},\dots,A_{N/k}
occur. When
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma>L
, let
\tilde{\psi}
instead be the indicator variable that takes value
1
if at least
\tfrac{N}{2k}-\tfrac{N}{k}\cdot\alpha(1-\varrho)
of
A_{1},\dots,A_{N/k}
occur.

Upon squaring and repeating the algebra from
Neykov [2022, Lemma II.5]
, the expressions in the definition of
A_{j}
satisfy

\displaystyle\bigg\|k^{-1}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}(\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i})-\nu_{1}\bigg\|^{2}-\bigg\|k^{-1}\sum_{i\in G_{j}}(\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i})-\nu_{2}\bigg\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|\left[(-1+2/C)\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|+\frac{2(\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu)^{T}(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})}{\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|}\right].

(C.3)

The probability that the first line above is bigger than
0
(i.e.,
A_{j}
occurs) is smaller than the probability (
C.3
) is bigger than 0, which is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{k}(\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu)^{T}v\geq\tfrac{\sqrt{k}\delta(C-2)}{2}\right)

(C.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(0\leq\sqrt{k}(\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu)^{T}v\leq\tfrac{\sqrt{k}\delta(C-2)}{2}\right),

where we used
\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|\geq C\delta
in the first claim and the symmetry of
\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu
in second one, and we set
v=(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})/\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|
for brevity.

Case 1:
Suppose

\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma\leq L
.

Let us show

\sup_{\mu:\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(A_{j}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{C_{2}\delta}{\sigma}.

(C.5)

where
C_{2}>0
is the universal constant from the proof of Lemma
C.2
. Well, by Lemma
C.1
, which is based on a local central limit theorem from
Petrov [1976, Chapter VII, Theorem 10]
, and the definition of
L,C_{2}
in Lemma
C.2
, we conclude that since
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma<L,
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(0\leq\sqrt{k}(\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu)^{T}v\leq\sqrt{k}\delta\cdot\tfrac{C-2}{2}\right)\geq\tfrac{\delta(C-2)}{2\sigma D_{2}}=\tfrac{C_{2}\delta}{\sigma}.

Combined with (
C.4
), we obtain (
C.5
).

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta
. Let us prove that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
by showing
\tilde{\psi}=0
implies
\psi=0
. Well, if
\tilde{\psi}=0
, then no more than
N/(2k)-(C_{2}\delta/\sigma)\cdot N/(2k)
of
A_{1},\dots,A_{N/k}
occur. Observe that if none of the
\tilde{X}_{i}
in a group
G_{j}
was corrupted, then
A_{j}
occurs if and only if
B_{j}
does. The only way for
A_{j}
to occur but not
B_{j}
or vice-versa is if one of the
\tilde{X}_{i}
in
G_{j}
was corrupted. Since at most
N\epsilon
of the
\tilde{X}_{i}
was corrupted and this is less than the number of groups (
N\epsilon\leq N\gamma/k<N/k
), this means at most
N\epsilon
of the groups are corrupted (i.e., contain a corrupted datapoint). Thus, at most

\displaystyle N/(2k)-(C_{2}\delta/\sigma)\cdot N/(2k)+N\epsilon

\displaystyle<N/(2k)-C_{1}C_{2}\epsilon N/(2k)+N\epsilon

\displaystyle=N/(2k)+N\epsilon(1-C_{1}C_{2}/(2k))

\displaystyle=N/(2k)

of
B_{1},\dots,B_{N/k}
occur, using
\delta/\sigma>C_{1}\epsilon
in the first inequality and
C_{1}C_{2}=2k
in the last line (from the proof of Lemma
C.2
). Thus,
\psi=0
, proving our claim that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
.

Now,
\tilde{\psi}=1
means no more than
N/(2k)+(C_{2}\delta/\sigma)\cdot N/(2k)
of
A_{1}^{c},\dots,A_{N/k}^{c}
occur. Set
\zeta=C_{2}\delta/(2\sigma)
and
p=1/2+C_{2}\delta/\sigma
, so that
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j}^{c})\geq p
and
p-\zeta=1/2+C_{2}\delta/(2\sigma)
. Then Hoeffding’s bound implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N/k,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j}^{c}))\leq N/(2k)+(C_{2}\delta/\sigma)\cdot N/(2k))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N/k,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j}^{c}))\leq(N/k)(p-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N/k,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j}^{c}))\leq(N/k)(\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j}^{c})-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-2N\zeta^{2}/k)

\displaystyle=\exp(-\tfrac{C_{2}^{2}N\delta^{2}}{2k\sigma^{2}}).

Taking the supremum over
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta
finishes the claim in this first case.

Case 2:
Suppose

\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma>L
.

Well,
\sqrt{k}(\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu)^{T}v
is a mean
0
sub-Gaussian random variable with parameter
\sqrt{2}\sigma
. Then using the upper deviation inequality in
Wainwright [2019, Section 2.1.2, page 23]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{k}(\overline{\tilde{X}_{i}}+\overline{R_{i}}-\mu)^{T}v\geq\sqrt{k}\delta\cdot\tfrac{C-2}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right).

Now observe that given
x,t>0
, we have
\exp(-x)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\exp(-tx)
if
t\leq 1-\tfrac{\log 2}{x}
. Set
x=\frac{k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}
and set
t=\beta\in(0,1)
from Lemma
C.2
. Then since
\sqrt{k}\delta/\sigma>L
, one can verify
\beta\leq 1-\tfrac{\log 2}{x}
will hold. Thus, recalling the definition of
A_{j}
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{\beta k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)=\varrho/2,

noting we set
\varrho=\exp\left(-\frac{\beta k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)
.

Again, fix
\mu
such that
\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta
and we show
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
. Suppose
\tilde{\psi}=0
. Then no more than
N/2k-N\alpha(1-\varrho)/k
of
A_{1},\dots,A_{N/k}
occur. In the worst case, no more than
N\epsilon
of the groups (noting that
N\epsilon<N\gamma/k<N/k
) contain a corrupted point, hence no more than

\displaystyle N/(2k)-N\alpha(1-\varrho)/k+N\epsilon

\displaystyle=N/(2k)+N(\epsilon-\alpha(1-\varrho)/k)

\displaystyle\leq N/(2k)

of
B_{1},\dots,B_{N/k}
occur, using
k\epsilon<\alpha(1-\varrho)
from (ii) of Lemma
C.2
. Hence
\psi=0
, proving
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
.

To bound
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)
, we closely follow the calculations from the Gaussian case but with
N/k
instead. We set
p=1-\varrho/2
,
\zeta=(1/2-\alpha)(1-\varrho)
so that
p-\zeta=\tfrac{1}{2}+\alpha-\alpha\varrho
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j}^{c})\geq p
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N/k,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j}^{c})\leq N/(2k)+(N/k)\alpha(1-\varrho))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N/k,\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{j}^{c})\leq(N/k)(p-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N/k,p)\leq(N/k)(p-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{N}{k}\cdot D(p-\zeta\|p)\right),

using the same stochastic dominance and Chernoff bound argument from the Gaussian case. Recall we also derived

\displaystyle D(p-\zeta\|p)

\displaystyle\geq g(\alpha)+(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho),

where
g
is defined in Lemma
B.1
. Thus, it suffices to require as before that
(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho)\geq-2g(\alpha)
, as we did in (iii) of Lemma
C.2
. This will imply

\displaystyle D(p-\zeta\|p)

\displaystyle\geq(1/2)(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho).

Then since
\log(1/\varrho)=\tfrac{\beta k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\tilde{\psi}=1)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{N}{k}\cdot(1/2)(1/2-\alpha)\cdot\tfrac{\beta k\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\tfrac{(1/2-\alpha)\beta(C-2)^{2}}{32}\cdot\tfrac{N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

∎

C.2
Proofs: General sub-Gaussian Noise

First, we give a lemma stating the existence of some useful constants. Then we give two auxiliary results to establish our Type I error bound, where one handles the case where we used the trimmed mean estimator and the other our usual median-like estimator. Note that these two auxiliary results bound the first supremum term in the statement of Theorem
4.5
, but as explained at the end of the proof of Theorem
3.6
and at the start of the proof of Theorem
4.1
, we can apply a symmetry argument (with sub-Gaussian tail bounds instead) for the other supremum. This is enabled by our addition of the
R_{i}
so that the tail bounds are the same with strict or weak inequality.

Lemma C.3
.

There exists positive absolute constants
C,C_{1},C_{3},D_{2},\alpha\in(0,1/2)
with the following properties. Let
\sigma>0
, and
\epsilon\in(0,1/32)
be given. Let the function
g(t)
be defined as in Lemma
B.1
. Define
D_{1}=4\sqrt{2(C_{3}+\log 4)}
,
D_{3}=8+3D_{1}^{2}/8
,
D_{4}=D_{2}\sqrt{2D_{3}}+D_{1}
,
D_{6}=\sqrt{64D_{3}\log 2}
.
Pick any
\delta>0
. Set
\beta=1-\frac{64D_{3}\log 2}{(C-2)^{2}}
and denote
\varrho=\exp\left(-\frac{\beta\delta^{2}(C-2)^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)
. Define the following properties:

(i)

C-2>\max(D_{1},D_{4},D_{6})

(ii)

\tfrac{D_{2}D_{3}}{C_{1}(C-2-D_{1})}\in(0,1)

(iii)

\beta\in(0,1)

(iv)

(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho)\geq-2g(\alpha)

(v)

\epsilon<\alpha(1-\varrho)

Then (i)-(iii) always hold, and if
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq D_{3}^{-1}/4
, (iv) and (v) hold.

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

We take
D_{2}
to be an absolute constant that bounded
\mathcal{E}
from
Lugosi and Mendelson [2021, Theorem 1]
using moment properties of sub-Gaussian random variables (see the remarks following their theorem). Pick
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
such that

\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)\right)>1/32,

(C.6)

noting this is possible by (
ix
) of Lemma
B.1
. Pick
C_{3}>0
arbitrarily. This lets us define
D_{1},D_{3},D_{4},D_{6}
. Note that
D_{3}
is the same quantity that appeared in Definition
4.4
.

Observe that
\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\frac{-2g(x)}{1/2-x}=\log 4
and
\lim_{x\to{1/2}^{-}}\frac{-2g(x)}{1/2-\alpha}=\infty
((
iii
) and (
iv
) of Lemma
B.1
).
So given our choice of
\alpha
, pick
C
large enough such that (
i
) holds and

\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}-64D_{3}\log 2}{64D_{3}}\geq-\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}.

(C.7)

After that, pick
C_{1}>\tfrac{D_{2}D_{3}}{C-2-D_{1}}
, noting the denominator is positive by (
i
).

Then note that (
i
) implies the quantity appearing in (
ii
) is positive, and our requirement
C_{1}>\tfrac{D_{2}D_{3}}{C-2-D_{1}}
ensures it is
<1
, yielding the claim (
ii
). Moreover, (
iii
) follows from (
i
) since
C-2>D_{6}
implies
\beta>0
and clearly
\beta<1
.

Now suppose
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq D_{3}^{-1}/4
. Observe our assumption on
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
implies

\displaystyle\tfrac{\beta(C-2)^{2}\delta^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\tfrac{64D_{3}\log 2}{(C-2)^{2}}\right)\tfrac{\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}}{16}=\left(\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}}{16}-\tfrac{64D_{3}\log 2}{16}\right)\cdot\tfrac{\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}}{16}-\tfrac{64D_{3}\log 2}{16}\right)\cdot\tfrac{1}{4D_{3}}=\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}-64D_{3}\log 2}{64D_{3}}

where the numerator is positive since
C-2>D_{6}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle 1-\varrho

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}-64D_{3}\log 2}{64D_{3}}\right).

(C.8)

Note that (
iv
) is equivalent to
1-\varrho\geq 1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)
. Due to (
C.8
), it suffices to require

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}-64D_{3}\log 2}{64D_{3}}\right)\leq\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)

which we have from (
C.7
). So (
iv
)
holds, hence
\alpha(1-\varrho)\geq\alpha\left(1-\exp\left(\tfrac{2g(\alpha)}{1/2-\alpha}\right)\right)
. But recalling (
C.6
) and
\epsilon<1/32
, we obtain (
v
).
∎

Lemma C.4
.

There exists positive absolute constants
C>2,C_{1}>0,C_{3}>0
with the following property. Let
\sigma>0
and
\epsilon\in(0,1/32)
. Suppose
\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|\geq C\delta
for
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in K
and
\mu\in K
is such that
\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta
. Suppose
\delta>0
is such
\delta/\sigma\geq C_{1}\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
and additionally that
N^{-1}\leq\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq D_{3}^{-1}/4
where
D_{3}=8+12\log 4+12C_{3}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})>0)\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{C_{3}N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

Proof.

Let
C
,
C_{1}
,
C_{3}
, and each of the
D_{i}
be taken from Lemma
C.3
. Recalling (
4.2
), we may write for an uncorrupted
V_{i}
that

\displaystyle V_{i}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\mu)^{T}v-(C-2)\delta

where
v=\tfrac{\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}}{\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|}
. Hence, using both (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), an uncorrupted
V_{i}
is sub-Gaussian with mean
m
where
m\leq-(C-2)\delta
.

Using
Lugosi and Mendelson [2021, Theorem 1]
(see also
Diakonikolas and Kane [2023, Proposition 1.18]
for a related result), we may bound the deviation between this uncorrupted mean
m
and the trimmed mean estimator applied to the post-corruption data. The theorem states with probability
1-\delta_{0}=1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{C_{3}N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)
that

\displaystyle|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|\leq 3\mathcal{E}(4\tilde{\epsilon},V)+2\sigma_{X}\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(4/\delta_{0})}{N}},

(C.9)

where
\mathcal{E}
is defined in Section 2 of their paper. As explained in the main text, the assumption
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq D_{3}^{-1}/4\leq C_{3}^{-1}
implies their
\delta_{0}\geq e^{-N}/4
condition (in addition
\delta_{0}\leq 1
so that
\log(4/\delta_{0})>0
). Moreover,
\tilde{\epsilon}\in(0,1/2)
from our
\epsilon\leq 1/32
requirement as explained earlier, so
\tilde{\epsilon}
is a valid quantile for use in the trimmed mean algorithm.

For the second term in (
C.9
), expanding
\delta_{0}
we have

\displaystyle 2\sigma_{X}\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(4/\delta_{0})}{N}}

\displaystyle=2\sigma_{X}\sqrt{\tfrac{\log 4}{N}+\tfrac{C_{3}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}}\leq 4\sqrt{2}\sigma\sqrt{\tfrac{\log 4}{N}+\tfrac{C_{3}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}}

\displaystyle=4\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\tfrac{\sigma^{2}\log 4}{N}+C_{3}\delta^{2}}\leq 4\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\delta^{2}\log 4+C_{3}\delta^{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta\cdot 4\sqrt{2(C_{3}+\log 4)}=D_{1}\delta,

where we used
\sigma_{X}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\sigma
and
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq 1/N
and recall
D_{1}=4\sqrt{2(C_{3}+\log 4)}
.

For the other term in (
C.9
), we use the authors’ second remark after their Theorem 1 for the sub-Gaussian case with some convenient re-scaling. Namely, there exists an absolute constant
D_{2}>0
(which we used in Lemma
C.3
) such that
3\mathcal{E}(4\tilde{\epsilon},V)\leq D_{2}\sigma\tilde{\epsilon}\sqrt{\log(1/\tilde{\epsilon})}
. This fact can be seen with a sub-Gaussian moment bound and Hölder’s inequality. Also,
\tilde{\epsilon}>\epsilon
so
\sqrt{\log(1/\tilde{\epsilon})}\leq\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
. Then expanding
\tilde{\epsilon}
and using this fact along with
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq 1/N
,

\displaystyle 3\mathcal{E}(4\tilde{\epsilon},V)

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\sigma\tilde{\epsilon}\sqrt{\log(1/\tilde{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\sigma(8\epsilon+12\tfrac{\log(4/\delta_{0})}{N})\cdot\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}

\displaystyle=D_{2}\sigma(8\epsilon+\tfrac{12\log 4}{N}+\tfrac{12C_{3}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})\cdot\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\sigma(8\epsilon+\tfrac{12\delta^{2}\log 4}{\sigma^{2}}+\tfrac{12C_{3}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})\cdot\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}

\displaystyle=D_{2}\sigma(8\epsilon+\tfrac{3D_{1}^{2}\delta^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}})\cdot\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}.

Consider two sub-cases. In the first assume
\epsilon>\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
. Then continuing from the previous line,

\displaystyle 3\mathcal{E}(4\tilde{\epsilon},V)

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\sigma(8\epsilon+\tfrac{3\epsilon D_{1}^{2}}{8})\cdot\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}=D_{2}D_{3}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)},

noting
D_{3}=8+3D_{1}^{2}/8=8+12C_{3}+12\log 4
.

On the other hand, suppose
\epsilon\leq\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
. Then using this along with
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq N^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=8\epsilon+\tfrac{12\log 4}{N}+\tfrac{12C_{3}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\leq\tfrac{8\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+\tfrac{12\log 4\cdot\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+\tfrac{12C_{3}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}=\tfrac{D_{3}\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}.

Now the map
x\mapsto x\sqrt{\log(1/x)}
is real-valued, positive, upper-bounded by
1
on
(0,1)
, and moreover, it is increasing on
(0,1/2]
. It also has limit
0
as
x\to 0^{+}
. Observe that
D_{3}\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq D_{3}\cdot(D_{3}^{-1}/4)=1/4
by our assumption on
\delta/\sigma
so we may apply monotonicity to the inputs
\tilde{\epsilon}\leq D_{3}\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq 1/4
. This inequality also shows
D_{3}^{1/2}\delta/\sigma\in(0,1)
, so the upper bound
(D_{3}^{1/2}\delta/\sigma)\sqrt{\log(D_{3}^{-1/2}\sigma/\delta)}\leq 1
applies.

Thus, using monotonicity on
(0,1/2]
, pulling out a factor of
2
from the logarithm, and applying the upper bound on
(D_{3}^{1/2}\delta/\sigma)\sqrt{\log(D_{3}^{-1/2}\sigma/\delta)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle 3\mathcal{E}(4\tilde{\epsilon},V)

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\sigma\tilde{\epsilon}\sqrt{\log(1/\tilde{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\sigma\cdot(D_{3}\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2})\cdot\sqrt{\log(D_{3}^{-1}\sigma^{2}/\delta^{2})}

\displaystyle=D_{2}\sqrt{D_{3}}\delta\cdot(\sqrt{D_{3}}\delta/\sigma)\cdot\sqrt{2\log(D_{3}^{-1/2}\sigma/\delta)}

\displaystyle\leq D_{2}\sqrt{2D_{3}}\delta,

noting all logarithm terms are positive.
To summarize, if

\max(N^{-1},C_{1}^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon))\leq\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq D_{3}^{-1}/4,

then with probability at least
1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{C_{3}N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(V_{1},\dots,V_{2N})-m|\leq\begin{cases}D_{1}\delta+D_{2}D_{3}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}&\text{ if }\epsilon>\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\\
D_{4}\delta&\text{ if }\epsilon\leq\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2},\end{cases}

(C.10)

where
D_{1}=4\sqrt{2(C_{3}+\log 4)}
,
D_{2}
is some absolute constant from sub-Gaussian properties,
D_{3}=8+3D_{1}^{2}/8
, and
D_{4}=D_{2}\sqrt{2D_{3}}+D_{1}
. Recall from Lemma
C.3
that
C-2>\max(D_{1},D_{4})
.

Then, since
m\leq-(C-2)\delta
and
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq D_{3}^{-1}/4
by assumption,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\psi(\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})=1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})>0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m>-m)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m>(C-2)\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|>(C-2)\delta).

(C.11)

For the
\epsilon\leq\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
case, since
C-2>D_{4},
we have from (
C.10
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|>(C-2)\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|>D_{4}\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{C_{3}N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

Consider the
\epsilon>\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}
case. Recall we assumed
\delta\geq C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
. Then the following can be seen by re-arranging:

\displaystyle\delta-\tfrac{D_{2}D_{3}}{C-2-D_{1}}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}\geq\left(1-\tfrac{D_{2}D_{3}}{C_{1}(C-2-D_{1})}\right)\delta=D_{5}\delta

(C.12)

where we set
D_{5}=1-\frac{D_{2}D_{3}}{C_{1}(C-2-D_{1})}.
Note that
D_{5}\in(0,1)
by (ii) of Lemma
C.3
. Thus, returning to (
C.11
), adding and subtracting some terms, using (
C.12
), applying
C-2>D_{1}
from (i) of Lemma
C.3
, and finally using (
C.10
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|>(C-2)\delta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|>D_{1}\delta+D_{2}D_{3}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(C-2-D_{1})\delta-D_{2}D_{3}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|>D_{1}\delta+D_{2}D_{3}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(C-2-D_{1})(\delta-\tfrac{D_{2}D_{3}}{C-2-D_{1}}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|>D_{1}\delta+D_{2}D_{3}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(C-2-D_{1})D_{5}\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\mathrm{TM}_{\delta_{0}}(\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2N})-m|>D_{1}\delta+D_{2}D_{3}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{C_{3}N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

∎

Lemma C.5
.

There exist sufficiently large absolute constants
C>2,C_{1}>0,C_{3}>0,C_{4}>0
and
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
with the following properties. Let
\sigma>0
and
\epsilon\in(0,1/32)
. Suppose
\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|\geq C\delta
for
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in K
and
\mu\in K
is such that
\|\mu-\nu_{1}\|\leq\delta
. Suppose
\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|\geq C\delta
for
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in K
. Suppose
\delta>0
is such
\delta/\sigma\geq C_{1}\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
and additionally that
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq D_{3}^{-1}/4
where
D_{3}=8+12\log 4+12C_{3}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|\{i\in[2N]:\|X_{i}-\nu_{1}\|\geq\|X_{i}-\nu_{2}\|\}|\geq N)\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{C_{4}N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

Proof.

Let
C
,
C_{1}
,
C_{3}
,
\alpha
, and the
D_{i}
be given from Lemma
C.3
. We borrow arguments from the Gaussian case. We set
A_{i}
to be the event
\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\nu_{1}\|\geq\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\nu_{2}\|
and
B_{i}
the event
\|X_{i}-\nu_{1}\|\geq\|X_{i}-\nu_{2}\|
, where recall
\tilde{X}_{i}
is the uncorrupted data and
X_{i}
is the corrupted data (and we added a Gaussian noise term
R_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I})
in either case). Recalling the computations in (
4.2
), we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\nu_{1}\|^{2}-\|\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\nu_{2}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\mu)^{T}(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})+(-1+2/C)\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|^{2},

noting this is a sub-Gaussian random variable with mean
m=(-1+2/C)\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|^{2}\leq(2-C)\delta<0
and parameter
\sqrt{8}\sigma\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|
(noting the addition of a Gaussian noise term multiples the parameter by
\sqrt{2}
). Using
\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|\geq C\delta
and the upper deviation inequality from
Wainwright [2019, Section 2.1.2]
for sub-Gaussian random variables,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(\|\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\nu_{1}\|^{2}-\|\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\nu_{2}\|^{2}>0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(2(\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\mu)^{T}(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})>(1-2/C)\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(2(\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\mu)^{T}(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})>(C-2)\delta\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}\delta^{2}\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|^{2}}{2\cdot 8\sigma^{2}\|\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}\delta^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right).

Recall once more that for any
x,t>0
, we have
\exp(-x)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\exp(-tx)
provided
t\leq 1-\tfrac{\log 2}{x}
. Well setting
x=\tfrac{(C-2)^{2}\delta^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}
and
t=1-\tfrac{64D_{3}\log 2}{(C-2)^{2}}
(which is positive so long as
C>2+\sqrt{64D_{3}\log 2}
, guaranteed from (
i
) of Lemma
C.3
), one can verify
t\leq 1-\tfrac{\log 2}{x}
indeed holds since
\delta^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq D_{3}^{-1}/4
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\left(\|\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\nu_{1}\|^{2}-\|\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}-\nu_{2}\|^{2}>0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\tfrac{\beta(C-2)^{2}\delta^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)=\varrho/2

where we set
\beta=1-\tfrac{64D_{3}\log 2}{(C-2)^{2}}
and
\varrho=\exp\left(-\tfrac{\beta(C-2)^{2}\delta^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}\right)
. We will have
\beta\in(0,1)
from Lemma
C.3
.

From here, the proof is completely identical to the second case of the Gaussian setting’s testing result in Theorem
3.6
once we had established
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(A_{i})\leq\varrho/2
, just with a different
\varrho
and we use
\tilde{X}_{i}+R_{i}
and
X_{i}+R_{i}
instead of just
\tilde{X}_{i}
and
X_{i}
. We still require the same assumptions that
\epsilon<\alpha(1-\varrho)
and that
(1/2-\alpha)\log(1/\varrho)\geq-2g(\alpha)
where
g
was defined in Lemma
B.1
and
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
is some absolute constant. But these will hold by Lemma
C.3
. Following the last few computations in the proof of Theorem
3.6
, our ultimate bound on the Type I error will be

\exp\left(-N\cdot\tfrac{1/2-\alpha}{2}\cdot\log(1/\varrho)\right)=\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\alpha)\beta(C-2)^{2}\delta^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right)=\exp(-\tfrac{C_{4}N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}),

where
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
is some absolute constant,
\beta=1-\tfrac{64D_{3}\log 2}{(C-2)^{2}}\in(0,1)
, and we set
C_{4}=\frac{(1/2-\alpha)\beta(C-2)^{2}}{32}.

∎

We split the proof of Theorem
4.7
into lemmas as in the Gaussian case. We start with the analogues of Lemma
B.6
and Lemma
B.7
with Lemma
C.6
and Lemma
C.7
, respectively. We omit the proofs of both lemmas as they are near replicas of the Gaussian counterpart. The main distinction is that we swap out the hypothesis on
\delta/\sigma
. That is, we assume
\delta/\sigma\gtrsim\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
rather than
\delta/\sigma\gtrsim\epsilon
, and we additionally require
\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}\leq\delta
, where in this context
\delta=\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}
. The last distinction is that our bound is on
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}
as explained in Remark
4.3
. Otherwise there are no changes to the underlying logic.

Lemma C.6
.

Let
\eta_{J}
be defined as in Theorem
4.7
and let
C_{1},C_{3},C_{5}
be given from Theorem
4.5
. Suppose
\tilde{J}
is such that (
4.3
) holds, that
\frac{d}{2^{\tilde{J}-1}(C+1)}\geq(C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)})\vee\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
. Then for each
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|\geq\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}}\right)\leq 2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

Lemma C.7
.

Let
\nu^{\ast\ast}
denote the output after at least
J^{\ast}
iterations. Set
C_{2}=\tfrac{(19+16C)^{2}}{4C_{5}}
. Then under the same assumptions as in Lemma
C.6
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\leq C_{2}\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4C_{2}\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

We may now proceed with proof of Theorem
4.7
by handling the scenarios when these assumptions on
\delta/\sigma
in the previous two lemmas fail. In all cases, we end up dealing with a bound of the form in Lemma
C.7
but at indices possibly prior to
J^{\ast}
, like in the Gaussian case. The analogous non-corrupted setting of
Neykov [2022]
did not require this case-by-case analysis, by contrast.

Proof of Theorem
4.7
.

We re-use the same notation from the proof of Theorem
3.8
. If
J^{\ast}=1
, then the claimed upper bound reduces to
d^{2}
and trivially holds. Suppose
J^{\ast}>1
.

Case 1:
Suppose we have that
\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\ast}-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
and moreover that
\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}\leq\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\ast}-1}(C+1)}
. Here
C_{1}
is chosen from Theorem
4.5
. Then by Lemma
C.7
, we will obtain

\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\leq C_{2}\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4C_{2}\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)

where
\nu^{\ast\ast}
is the output after at least
J^{\ast}
steps. Note the use of
\mathbb{E}_{R}
in Lemma
C.7
, in line with the discussion from Remark
4.3
. Since
N\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}>\log 2
from the definition of
J^{\ast}
, we have
\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}<\frac{\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}}{\log 2}
and the exponential is of constant order, so the bound on
\mathbb{E}_{X}\mathbb{E}_{R}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}
is of order
\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}
, meaning it is bounded by
\max(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon))
. The bound from
d^{2}
is immediate, completing this case.

Let us now handle the remaining scenarios that would prevent the above argument from working. There are one of two assumptions that could go wrong. We could either obtain a
J\in\{1,2,\dots,J^{\ast}\}
such that
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}<C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
or such that
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}<\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
.

Case 2:
Suppose
\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime}-1}(C+1)}<\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
at some
1\leq J^{\prime}\leq J^{\ast}
(chosen minimally).

Case 2(a)
: If
J^{\prime}=1
, this means
d\lesssim\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
. But this implies
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\lesssim d^{2}\asymp\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\wedge d^{2}
. Now we must have
\eta_{J^{\ast}}\geq\sigma\sqrt{\log 2}/\sqrt{N}
by (
4.3
) since
J^{\ast}>1
. Thus

\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\wedge d^{2}\lesssim\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}\leq\max(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon))\wedge d^{2}.

(C.13)

Indeed
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\lesssim\max(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon))\wedge d^{2}
as claimed.

Case 2(b)
: Suppose
J^{\prime}\geq 2
. By minimality, this means for
1\leq J\leq J^{\prime}-1
, we have
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}\geq\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
. Now consider two sub-cases.

Case 2(b)(i):
In the first, say for some
J^{\prime\prime}\leq J^{\prime}-1
chosen minimally, we have
\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime\prime}-1}(C+1)}<C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
. If
J^{\prime\prime}=1
, that means
d\lesssim\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
which means

\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\lesssim d^{2}\lesssim\max(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon))\wedge d^{2}.

We proceed to the case
J^{\prime\prime}>1
. That means for
1\leq J\leq J^{\prime\prime}-1
, noting
J^{\prime\prime}\leq J^{\prime}-1
, we have both
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}\geq\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
and
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
. We may therefore apply Lemma
C.7
with
\tilde{J}=J^{\prime\prime}-1
to obtain

\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\leq C_{2}\eta_{J^{\prime\prime}-1}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4C_{2}\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J^{\prime\prime}-1}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

(C.14)

Consider two sub-cases. First, say
\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}\leq\tfrac{C_{6}}{\sqrt{N}}
where
0<C_{6}\leq\tfrac{\sqrt{\log 2}}{C_{1}\sqrt{C_{5}}}
. Then by definition of
J^{\prime\prime}
we have

\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime\prime}-1}}<(C+1)C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}\leq(C+1)C_{1}C_{6}\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}.

Rearranging,

\tfrac{N\eta_{J^{\prime\prime}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\leq[C_{1}\sqrt{C_{5}}C_{6}]^{2}\leq\log 2.

Thus, (
4.3
) does not hold, which means
J^{\prime\prime}\geq J^{\ast}
. But we assumed
J^{\prime\prime}\leq J^{\prime}-1<J^{\ast}
, so this is a contradiction.

Suppose instead
\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}\geq\tfrac{C_{6}}{\sqrt{N}}
. This implies
\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\lesssim\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)
and the exponential is always
\leq 1
. So the entire second term of (
C.14
) is
\lesssim\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)
. Moreover,
\eta_{J^{\prime\prime}-1}=2\eta_{J^{\prime\prime}}\asymp\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime\prime}-1}}\lesssim\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
by definition of
J^{\prime\prime}
. So (
C.14
) is bounded by
\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)
which is clearly less than
\max(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon))
. The upper bound of
d^{2}
is trivial, so we obtain the desired bound on
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}
.

Case 2(b)(ii)
: In the other sub-case, we have
\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime}-1}(C+1)}<\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
, while for
1\leq J\leq J^{\prime}-1
both
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}\geq\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
and
\tfrac{d}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
. In other words, only the
\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
condition fails, while in Case 2(b)(i) both this and the
\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
condition failed at some point.

We may therefore apply Lemma
C.7
with
\tilde{J}=J^{\prime}-1
to conclude

\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\leq C_{2}\eta_{J^{\prime}-1}^{2}+\mathbbm{1}(J^{\ast}>1)\cdot 4C_{2}\cdot\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J^{\prime}-1}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

(C.15)

Then observe that
\eta_{J^{\prime}-1}=2\eta_{J^{\prime}}<2\sqrt{C_{5}}\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
by assumption of this Case 2(b)(ii). The bound in (
C.15
) becomes
\lesssim\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}+\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J^{\prime}-1}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)
. The exponential term is clearly
\leq 1
. Thus, (
C.15
) is
\lesssim\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}
. Hence
\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\lesssim\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\wedge d^{2}
. But recall our argument that
J^{\ast}>1
and (
4.3
) implies the bound (
C.13
). Hence

\mathbb{E}_{R}\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\lesssim\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\wedge d^{2}\lesssim\max(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon))\wedge d^{2}

as required.

Case 3
: The final consideration is if
\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime}-1}(C+1)}<\tfrac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}}
never occurs for
1\leq J^{\prime}\leq J^{\ast}
, but we do encounter
\tfrac{d}{2^{J^{\prime}-1}(C+1)}<C_{1}\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
at some
1\leq J^{\prime}\leq J^{\ast}
. Then we repeat the argument of Case 2(b)(i) with
J^{\prime}
in place of
J^{\prime\prime}
. To briefly summarize the argument, if
J^{\prime}=1
we bound
d\lesssim\sigma\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}
and the claim immediately follows. If not, apply Lemma
C.7
with
\tilde{J}=J^{\prime}-1
. Split into two subcases on whether
\epsilon\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}\leq\tfrac{C_{6}}{\sqrt{N}}
or not. In the first sub-case, we end up concluding
J^{\ast}=1
(theorem trivially holds) and in the second sub-case, we bound the expectation with
\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)
and the rest of the bound easily follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.8
.

As we argued in the Gaussian case, the
\eta^{\ast}=0
case implies
d=0
, and the minimax rate is trivially achieved. We henceforth assume
\eta^{\ast}>0
and thus that
d>0
, so that
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta,c)
can be made arbitrarily large by taking
c
arbitrarily large.

Case 1:

N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}>8\log 2
.

We start with the lower bound. Recall Lemma
2.1
still applies in the sub-Gaussian setting. Repeating the argument from Case 1 of Theorem
3.10
, one can verify

\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\eta^{\ast}/4,c)\geq 4\left(\tfrac{N(\eta^{\ast}/4)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\vee\log 2\right),

so again applying Lemma
2.1
, we conclude
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}
and therefore
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}
is a lower bound up to constants. But also by Lemma
2.3
,
\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\wedge d^{2}
is a lower bound. Hence
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\wedge d^{2})
is a lower bound as claimed.

For the upper bound, we again repeat the argument from Case 1 of Theorem
3.10
. That is, observe from Theorem
4.7
that
\max(\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\wedge d^{2})
is an upper bound, so it suffices to obtain
\tilde{\eta}\asymp\eta^{\ast}
such that
{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}\geq\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}
. So set
\beta^{\prime}=\min(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\tfrac{c}{2}\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{C_{5}}})\in(0,1/\sqrt{2}]
, pick
D>1
such that
D\beta^{\prime}>1
, and take
\tilde{\eta}=D\sqrt{2}\eta^{\ast}
. One can verify as before that
\tilde{\eta}
satisfies (
4.3
) (with
c
, not
2c
by the logic in Remark
3.9
) using our
N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}>8\log 2
assumption. Then define a similar
\phi
(with
C_{5}
instead of
C_{3}(\kappa)
) and deduce
{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}\geq\eta_{J^{\ast}}^{2}
. Thus,
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\wedge d^{2})
is both a lower and upper bound up to constants.

Case 2:

N{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq 8\log 2
.

Repeat the argument of Case 3 of Theorem
3.10
. Namely, argue that a line segment of length
4\eta^{\ast}
cannot fit inside
K
, hence
d/3\leq 4\eta^{\ast}\leq 8\sigma\sqrt{2(\log 2)/N}
. Then
\tilde{\eta}=d/6
satisfies
2N{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq 64\log 2
while for sufficiently large
c
we can obtain
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tilde{\eta},c)>2N{\tilde{\eta}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}\vee 4\log 2
. Thus, by Lemma
2.1
, for sufficiently large
c
the minimax rate is lower bounded by
d^{2}
and hence
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}
. Combined with Lemma
2.3
, we conclude
\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\wedge d^{2})
is a lower bound. For the upper bound, note that
d^{2}
is trivially an upper bound, and we argued earlier that
d^{2}\leq 12^{2}{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}
. Hence
{\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2}\leq\max({\eta^{\ast}}^{2}\wedge d^{2},\sigma^{2}\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\wedge d^{2})
is an upper bound, establishing the minimax rate.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs for Section
5

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

For brevity set
Y_{i}=\tilde{X}_{i}-\mu
, which is a Gaussian with mean 0 and variance
\sigma^{2}
(or sub-Gaussian with mean 0 and parameter bounded by
\sigma
). Pick any
t\in(0,1)
. Construct a maximal
t
-packing of the unit sphere
S^{n-1}
in
n
dimensions which has cardinality bounded by
(1+\tfrac{2}{t})^{n}
by
Vershynin [2018, Corollary 4.2.13]
. Now pick any unit vector
v
in this packing. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|v^{T}Y_{i}|>R)\leq\exp(-\tfrac{R^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

Apply a union bound over the packing set, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\sup_{v}|v^{T}Y_{i}|>R)\leq(1+\tfrac{2}{t})^{n}\exp(-\tfrac{R^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

Now since we have a packing of
S^{n-1}
, for some
u\in S^{n-1}
we have
\|u-Y_{i}/\|Y_{i}\|\|\leq t
. Therefore

\displaystyle|u^{T}Y_{i}-\|Y_{i}\||

\displaystyle=|u^{T}Y_{i}-\|Y_{i}\|\cdot u^{T}u|=\|Y_{i}\|\cdot\big|u^{T}\big(\tfrac{Y_{i}}{\|Y_{i}\|}-u\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\|Y_{i}\|\cdot\|u\|\cdot\underbrace{\big\|\tfrac{Y_{i}}{\|Y_{i}\|}-u\big\|}_{\leq t}\leq t\|Y_{i}\|.

This means
(1-t)\|Y_{i}\|\leq u^{T}Y_{i}
for some
u
in our packing set. So

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}((1-t)\|Y_{i}\|>R)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\exists u:u^{T}Y_{i}>R)\leq\mathbb{P}(\sup_{v}|v^{T}Y_{i}|>R)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\tfrac{2}{t})^{n}\exp(-\tfrac{R^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}).

Take
t=1/2
, then we have
\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{i}\|>2R)\leq 5^{n}\exp(-\tfrac{R^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}})
. Performing a change of variables by replacing
R
with
R/2
, the bound becomes
\mathbb{P}(\|Y_{i}\|>R)\leq 5^{n}\exp(-\tfrac{R^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}})
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Recall
\tilde{E}_{\mu,i}
is the event that
\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\mu\|>R
. We know
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{E}_{\mu,i})\leq\exp(-\tfrac{\gamma R^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}})
from (
5.2
). Observe that if
x>2\log 2
and we take
\beta=1-\tfrac{\log 2}{x}\in(1/2,1)
, we have
\exp(-x)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\exp(-\beta x)
. Thus, take
x=\tfrac{\gamma R^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}
and
\beta=1-\tfrac{8\sigma^{2}\log 2}{\gamma R^{2}}
, noting that
\tfrac{\gamma R^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}>2\log 2
follows from the third bound in (
5.1
). Then
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{E}_{\mu,i})\leq\varrho/2
where
\varrho=\exp(-\tfrac{\beta\gamma R^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}})
.

Let us now bound
\mathbb{P}(\mu\not\in S)
. Well, if
\mu\not\in S
, then at least
N/2
of the events
E_{\mu,1},\dots,E_{\mu,N}
occur, i.e.,
\|X_{i}-\mu\|>R
. That means at least
N/2-\epsilon N
of
\tilde{E}_{\mu,i}
(i.e.,
\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\mu\|>R
) occur. To see this: if strictly fewer than
N/2-\epsilon N
of the
\tilde{X}_{i}
satisfy
\|\tilde{X}_{i}-\mu\|>R
, and we can corrupt at most fraction
\epsilon
of the
\tilde{X}_{i}
, then strictly fewer than
N/2-\epsilon N+\epsilon N=N/2
of the
X_{i}
can satisfy
\|X_{i}-\mu\|>R
. This contradicts our assumption that at least
N/2
of the
E_{\mu,i}
occur. To summarize:

\mathbb{P}(\mu\not\in S)\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}(E_{\mu,i}))\geq N/2)\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}(\tilde{E}_{\mu,i}))\geq N/2-\epsilon N).

Next, note that the
\tilde{E}_{\mu,i}
are i.i.d. and
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{E}_{\mu,i})\leq\varrho/2
(so
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{E}_{\mu,i}^{c})\geq 1-\varrho/2
). Set
p=1-\varrho/2\in(1/2,1)
. Set
\zeta=p-1/2-\epsilon
, so
p-\zeta=1/2+\epsilon
. Then our probability is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}(\tilde{E}_{\mu,i}))\geq N/2-\epsilon N)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}(\tilde{E}_{\mu,i}^{c}))\leq N/2+\epsilon N)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,\mathbb{P}(\tilde{E}_{\mu,i}^{c}))\leq N(p-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(N,p)\leq N(p-\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-N\cdot D(p-\zeta\|p)\right),

where we repeat the reasoning leading up to (
B.9
) in the proof of Theorem
3.6
with stochastic dominance and a Chernoff bound.

Recalling the definition of
D(q\|p)
from (
B.9
), we compute

\displaystyle D((p-\zeta)\|p)

\displaystyle=(p-\zeta)\log\frac{p-\zeta}{p}+(1-(p-\zeta))\log\frac{1-(p-\zeta)}{1-p}

\displaystyle=(1/2+\epsilon)\log\frac{1/2+\epsilon}{1-\varrho/2}+(1/2-\epsilon)\log\frac{1/2-\epsilon}{\varrho/2}

\displaystyle\geq(1/2+\epsilon)\log(1/2+\epsilon)+(1/2-\epsilon)\log\frac{1/2-\epsilon}{\varrho/2}

\displaystyle=g(\epsilon)+(1/2-\epsilon)\log(1/\varrho),

where
g
was defined in Lemma
B.1
. In the third line, we used that
x\mapsto\tfrac{1/2+\epsilon}{1-x/2}
has derivative
\tfrac{2\epsilon+1}{(2-x)^{2}}
, so the map is increasing. Thus, we can lower bound the first term by setting
\varrho=0
.

If we can ensure
(1/2-\epsilon)\log(1/\varrho)\geq-2g(\epsilon)
like in Theorem
3.6
, we will have
D((p-\zeta)\|p)\geq(1/2)(1/2-\epsilon)\log(1/\varrho)
. This means our exponential term above will be bounded by

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{N(1/2)(1/2-\epsilon)\cdot\beta\gamma R^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma R^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right),

where we used that
\beta=1-\tfrac{8\sigma^{2}\log 2}{\gamma R^{2}}\in(1/2,1)
from our requirements on
R
, and
\gamma\in(0,1)
is just some constant.

Well, we can have
(1/2-\epsilon)\log(1/\varrho)\geq-2g(\epsilon)
if
\log(1/\varrho)\geq\frac{-2g(\epsilon)}{1/2-\epsilon}
. Using (
iii
), (
iv
), and (
v
) of Lemma
B.1
, we know
\frac{-2g(\epsilon)}{1/2-\epsilon}\in(\log 4,\infty)
. On the other hand,
\log(1/\varrho)=\tfrac{\beta\gamma R^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}>\tfrac{\gamma R^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}
using
\beta>1/2
. Thus,
\log(1/\varrho)\geq\frac{-2g(\epsilon)}{1/2-\epsilon}
follows by assuming
R>\sqrt{\frac{-32\sigma^{2}g(\epsilon)}{\gamma(1/2-\epsilon)}}
, as we did in the second requirement of (
5.1
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

Take any rational point
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
and
r=m/2
, and consider the closed Euclidean ball
B(p,r)
centered at
p
with radius
r
. Clearly
\cup_{p\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}}B(p,r)=\mathbb{R}^{n}
for any
r>0
. Exclude any
p
such that
B(p,r)\cap T=\varnothing
by letting
P=\{p\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}:B(p,r)\cap T\neq\varnothing\}
. Clearly
P
is countable and in addition
T\subset\cup_{p\in P}B(p,r)
.

Now for each
p\in P
, we are going to select a point
\hat{p}=\hat{p}(p)
from the set
B(p,r)\cap T
. Specifically, for each
p\in P
we will select the smallest point in the lexicographic ordering that belongs to these sets. First fix
p\in P
. Solve the following minimization problem:
\hat{p}_{1}:=\min_{\nu\in B(p,r)\cap T}\nu_{1}
where
\nu_{i}
represents the
i
th coordinate of
\nu
. The minimum is attained since the set
B(p,r)\cap T
is compact and
\nu\mapsto\nu_{1}
is a continuous function. Next, solve the minimization:
\hat{p}_{2}:=\min_{\nu\in B(p,r)\cap T,\nu_{1}=\hat{p}_{1}}\nu_{2}
. Observe that we have a minimization since the set
B(p,r)\cap T\cap\{\nu:\nu_{1}=\hat{p}_{1}\}
since subspaces are closed and the intersection of closed sets is closed. For the third coordinate, solve
\hat{p}_{3}:=\min_{\nu\in B(p,r)\cap T,\nu_{1}=\hat{p}_{1},\nu_{2}=\hat{p}_{2}}\nu_{3}
, and repeat this up to the
n
th coordinate. Then it is clear that the point
\hat{p}=(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\ldots,\hat{p}_{n})\in B(p,r)\cap T
(in fact, this is the smallest point lexicographically). Now consider
\cup_{p\in P}B(\hat{p},2r)
. This is a countable collection which covers
T
, and each point
\hat{p}\in T
. The covering claim follows since if
x\in T
, then
x\in B(p,r)
for some
p\in P
. But
B(p,r)\subset B(\hat{p},2r)
since for any
x\in B(p,r)
, the triangle inequality implies
\|x-\hat{p}\|\leq\|x-p\|+\|p-\hat{p}\|\leq 2r
. Hence, we have obtained a
2r=m
cover of
T
with points that are in
T
.

Now we prune this cover by the following procedure. Let
f
be a bijection from the set
\mathbb{N}
onto
F=\{\hat{p}:p\in P\}
. Set
L=\varnothing
. Our procedure is as follows: for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\inf_{l\in L}\|l-f(i)\|>m
(where infimum over the empty set is assumed to be
\infty
), then update
L=L\cup\{f(i)\}
.

Clearly this procedure produces a set
L
which is countable and an
m
-packing set. Now we verify that the set
L
satisfies the property that for any
x\in T
:
\inf_{l\in L}\|l-x\|\leq 2m
. Note that for any
x\in T
there exists a
\hat{p}
constructed from some
p\in P
such that
\|\hat{p}-x\|\leq m
. Now, if
\hat{p}\in L
there is nothing to prove. If
\hat{p}\not\in L
this means that
\hat{p}
was pruned at some step, which implies for any
\delta>0
the existence of a point
q\in L
such that
\|\hat{p}-q\|\leq m+\delta
so that
\|q-x\|\leq\|\hat{p}-q\|+\|\hat{p}-x\|\leq 2m+\delta
which completes the proof upon taking
\delta\rightarrow 0
.

Finally, we argue that the set
L
satisfies the definition of covering as defined in the statement. Above we only showed that for any
x\in T
that
\inf_{l\in L}\|l-x\|\leq 2m
. Consider the set
B(x,2m+\gamma)\cap T
for some small
\gamma
. Observe that there can only be finitely many points that belong to both this set and
L
since the points in
L
form an
m
-packing. On the other hand, by
\inf_{l\in L}\|l-x\|\leq 2m
, we know that there exists a point in
L
for any value of
\gamma
in the set
B(x,2m+\gamma)\cap T
. Since the set
B(x,2m)\cap T
is closed, it follows that there exists some
l\in L
which belongs to that set, proving that
\min_{l\in L}\|l-x\|\leq 2m
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.8
.

Recall
\Upsilon_{1}
is our chosen root node from the countable packing
S_{m}
, and
\Upsilon_{2}
is picked from a
d_{m}/c
-packing of
B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})\cap K
. Then for each
k\geq 3
,
\Upsilon_{k}
is picked by first taking a
\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-1}c}
-packing of
B(\Upsilon_{k-1},\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-2}})\cap K\cap B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})
and then possibly pruning the tree. If
\Upsilon_{k}
was pruned, that means for some
u
in this
\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-1}c}
-packing of
B(\Upsilon_{k-1},\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-2}})\cap K\cap B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})
, we have
\|\Upsilon_{k}-u\|\leq\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-1}c}
. By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\|\Upsilon_{k}-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\|\Upsilon_{k}-u\|+\|u-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-1}c}+\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-2}},

noting our bound on
\|u-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|
followed from
u\in B(\Upsilon_{k-1},\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-2}})
. If
\Upsilon_{k}
was not pruned,
\|\Upsilon_{k}-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-2}}
. Thus, for all
k\geq 3
, we have
\|\Upsilon_{k}-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-1}c}+\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-2}}
, and clearly
\|\Upsilon_{2}-\Upsilon_{1}\|\leq d_{m}
.

If
J\geq J^{\prime}\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle\|\Upsilon_{J}-\Upsilon_{J^{\prime}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=J^{\prime}+1}^{J}\|\Upsilon_{k}-\Upsilon_{k-1}\|\leq\sum_{k=J^{\prime}+1}^{J}\left[\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-1}c}+\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{k-2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2(2c+1)d_{m}}{c}\left[\tfrac{1}{2^{J^{\prime}}}-\tfrac{1}{2^{J}}\right]\leq\tfrac{d_{m}(2+4c)}{c2^{J^{\prime}}}.

If
J^{\prime}=1
and
J\geq 1
, then clearly
\|\Upsilon_{J}-\Upsilon_{1}\|\leq d_{m}
since both points belong to
K\cap B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})
, but
d_{m}\leq\frac{d_{m}(2+4c)}{c\cdot 2}
clearly holds. Therefore, for all
J\geq J^{\prime}\geq 1
, we have
\|\Upsilon_{J}-\Upsilon_{J^{\prime}}\|\leq\frac{d_{m}(2+4c)}{c2^{J^{\prime}}}
.
∎

The following lemma is essentially the same as Lemma
B.6
except we must condition everywhere on
S\neq\varnothing
and add an exponential to control
\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{1}-\mu\|>d_{m}|S\neq\varnothing)
, which we just showed is upper bounded by
\exp\left(-\Omega(NR^{2}/(8\sigma^{2}))\right)
, omitting some constants. The proof is also more complicated in that we bound
\mathbb{P}(A_{j}\cap A_{j-1}^{c}\cap A_{1}^{c}|S\neq\varnothing)
rather than
\mathbb{P}(A_{j}\cap A_{j-1}^{c}|S\neq\varnothing)
where
A_{j}
is the event
\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>d_{m}
. This is because we wish to take a union bound over all possible points in
S_{m}
(which is how we pick
\Upsilon_{1}
), while in the bounded setting, we know in advance which particular set to apply the algorithm to.

Lemma D.1
.

Consider the Gaussian noise setting. Let
\eta_{J}
be defined as in Theorem
5.9
. Suppose
\tilde{J}
is such (
5.4
) holds and also
\frac{d_{m}}{2^{\tilde{J}-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
. Let
C_{6}(\kappa)=\frac{\kappa\gamma C^{2}}{256C_{3}(\kappa)}
, an absolute constant depending only on
C
and
\kappa
. Then for each such
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{J-1}}|S\neq\varnothing\right)\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{NC_{6}(\kappa)\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)+4\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Proof of Lemma
D.1
.

If
\tilde{J}
satisfies the stated conditions, so will all
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
. For
j\geq 1
set
A_{j}=\{\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-1}}\}
. If a tree is rooted at a point
\Upsilon_{1}=s
, denote
\mathcal{L}^{s}(j)
to be the
j
th level of the directed tree rooted at that point.

Recall that our third requirement in (
5.1
) implies
R>\sqrt{\frac{32\sigma^{2}\log 2}{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma}}
which is equivalent to
\exp\left(-\frac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma R^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right)\leq 1/2
. Thus, using Lemma
5.3
,

\mathbb{P}(S\neq\varnothing)\geq\mathbb{P}(\mu\in S)\geq 1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma R^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right)\geq 1/2.

Now, our goal is to control
\mathbb{P}(A_{J}|S\neq\varnothing)
so we will apply (
B.10
) of Lemma
B.5
. Let us start with
\mathbb{P}(A_{j}\cap A_{j-1}^{c}\cap A_{1}^{c}|S\neq\varnothing)
for
j\geq 3
. Note that if
\|\Upsilon_{1}-\mu\|\leq d_{m}
, then
\Upsilon_{1}
belongs to a ball
B(\mu,d_{m})\cap S_{m}
, where recall
S_{m}
is our countable
m
-packing. Moreover, for the directed tree at
\Upsilon_{1}=s
, if
\Upsilon_{j-1}
at level
j-1
satisfies
\|\Upsilon_{j-1}-\mu\|\leq\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}}
, then
\Upsilon_{j-1}\in\mathcal{L}^{s}(j-1)\cap B(\mu,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})
. Thus, we can apply two union bounds and substitute the update rule for
\Upsilon_{j}
with
\delta=\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-1}(C+1)}
. Along the way, we will be conditioning on
S\neq\varnothing
, applying Bayes’ rule, then dropping it from the probability and using
\mathbb{P}(S\neq\varnothing)\geq 1/2
as established above.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A_{j}\cap A_{j-1}^{c}\cap A_{1}^{c}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(S\neq\varnothing)}\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-1}},\|\Upsilon_{j-1}-\mu\|\leq\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\|\Upsilon_{1}-\mu\|\leq d_{m},S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq 2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in S_{m}\\
\|s-\mu\|\leq d_{m}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{L}^{s}(j-1)\cap\\
B(\mu,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})\end{subarray}}\!\!\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{j}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-1}},\Upsilon_{j-1}=u,\Upsilon_{1}=s,S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle=2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in S_{m}\\
\|s-\mu\|\leq d_{m}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{L}^{s}(j-1)\cap\\
B(\mu,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})\end{subarray}}\!\!\mathbb{P}\bigg(\Big\|\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}(u)}T(\delta,\nu,\mathcal{O}(u))-\mu\Big\|>(C+1)\delta,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Upsilon_{j-1}=u,\Upsilon_{1}=s,S\neq\varnothing\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in S_{m}\\
\|s-\mu\|\leq d_{m}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{L}^{s}(j-1)\cap\\
B(\mu,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\Big\|\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}(u)}T(\delta,\nu,\mathcal{O}(u))-\mu\Big\|>(C+1)\delta\right).

By Lemma
5.7
,
\left|\mathcal{L}^{s}(j-1)\cap B(\mu,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})\right|
is bounded by
\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}},2c)
. Moreover, since
S_{m}
is an
m
-packing, the number of choices for
\Upsilon_{1}
in
B(\mu,d_{m})
is bounded by
\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d_{m},c^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}},c^{\prime})
where
c^{\prime}=\tfrac{d_{m}}{m}
. Recall we took
m=\tfrac{R}{c-1}
, so

c^{\prime}=\frac{2R+2m}{m}=\frac{2R}{m}+2=\frac{2R(c-1)}{R}+2=(2c-2)+2=2c.

For a fixed choice of
\Upsilon_{1}=s
and
u\in\mathcal{L}^{s}(j-1)\cap B(\mu,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})
, set
K^{\prime}=B(u,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})\cap K\cap B(s,d_{m})
. Observe by Lemma
5.6
that
\mathcal{O}(u)
is a
\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}c}=\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-1}(C+1)}=\delta
-covering of the set
B(u,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})\cap K\cap B(s,d_{m})
with cardinality bounded by
\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}},2c)
. Thus, by our tournament bound in Lemma
3.7
followed by our union bounds from earlier and bounds on the number of summands, we have for
j\geq 3
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A_{j}\cap A_{j-1}^{c}\cap A_{1}^{c}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq 2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in S_{m}\\
\|s-\mu\|\leq d_{m}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{L}^{s}(j-1)\cap\\
B(\mu,\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}})\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}},2c)\exp(-\tfrac{C_{3}(\kappa)N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left[\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}},2c)\right]^{3}\exp(-\tfrac{C_{3}(\kappa)N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})

\displaystyle=2\left[\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{j-2}},2c)\right]^{3}\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{j}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}).

Next, note that
A_{1}
is the event
\|\Upsilon_{1}-\mu\|>d_{m}
, which implies
\mu\not\in B(\Upsilon_{1},d_{m})\cap K
. By (
5.3
), we have

\mathbb{P}(A_{1}|S\neq\varnothing)\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma R^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right).

For
j=2
, we again apply a union bound over all possible
\Upsilon_{1}=s
, and note that
\Upsilon_{2}
is chosen from
\mathcal{O}(s)
. Set
\delta=\tfrac{d_{m}}{c}=\tfrac{d_{m}}{2(C+1)}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A_{2}\cap A_{1}^{c}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(S\neq\varnothing)}\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{2}-\mu\|>d_{m}/2,\|\Upsilon_{1}-\mu\|\leq d_{m}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq 2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in S_{m}\\
\|s-\mu\|\leq d_{m}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{2}-\mu\|\geq d_{m}/2,\Upsilon_{1}=s,S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle=2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in S_{m}\\
\|s-\mu\|\leq d_{m}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\Big\|\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}(s)}T(\delta,\nu,\mathcal{O}(s))-\mu\Big\|\geq\delta(C+1),\Upsilon_{1}=s,S\neq\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in S_{m}\\
\|s-\mu\|\leq d_{m}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\Big\|\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}(s)}T(\delta,\nu,\mathcal{O}(s))-\mu\Big\|\geq\delta(C+1)\right).

We again apply Lemma
3.7
with
K^{\prime}=B(s,d_{m})\cap K
which is covered by a
\delta=d_{m}/c
maximal packing set
\mathcal{O}(s)
(with no pruning). Noting that
|\mathcal{O}(s)|\leq\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d_{m},2c)
(see Lemma
5.6
), and repeating our argument about
s\in S_{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A_{2}\cap A_{1}^{c}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq 2\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in S_{m}\\
\|s-\mu\|\leq d_{m}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d_{m},2c)\exp\left(-\tfrac{C_{3}(\kappa)N\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left[\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d_{m},2c)\right]^{2}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{2}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left[\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d_{m},2c)\right]^{3}\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{2}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right),

since
\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(d_{m},2c)\geq 1
.

Therefore, for any
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
, we apply (
B.10
) (but conditional on
S\neq\varnothing
) and repeat the logic of Lemma
B.6
but with
\mathbb{P}(A_{1}|S\neq\varnothing)
now non-zero. We then have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{J-1}}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma R^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+2\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\cdot\left[\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\tfrac{c\eta_{J}}{\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}},2c\right)\right]^{3}\frac{a_{J}}{1-a_{J}}

where
a_{J}=\exp(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})
. But then as before, assuming (
5.4
) holds, we can bound the second term and conclude

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{J-1}}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma R^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right)+4\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Next, we can compute from
m=\tfrac{R}{c-1}
that
d_{m}=2R+2m=\tfrac{2c}{c-1}\cdot R
. Thus

\eta_{J}=\tfrac{d_{m}\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}{2^{J-1}(C+1)}=\tfrac{2c\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}{2^{J-1}(C+1)(c-1)}\cdot R=\tfrac{4\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}{2^{J-1}(2C+1)}\cdot R.

Consequently,
R=\tfrac{2^{J-1}(2C+1)}{4\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}\eta_{J}\geq\tfrac{C}{4\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}\eta_{J}
, and

\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma R^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma C^{2}\eta_{J}^{2}}{512\sigma^{2}C_{3}(\kappa)}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\kappa\gamma C^{2}\eta_{J}^{2}}{512\sigma^{2}C_{3}(\kappa)}\right).

The last step used that
1/2-\epsilon>\kappa
in our Gaussian setting. Finally, we set
C_{6}(\kappa)=\frac{\kappa\gamma C^{2}}{256C_{3}(\kappa)}
, we bound our probability by
\exp\left(-\frac{NC_{6}(\kappa)\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)
. This yields our claimed bound.
∎

In the bounded case, we used the equivalent of Lemma
D.1
to construct a bound on
\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\gtrsim x)
for all
x\geq\eta_{\tilde{J}}
. That proof relied on arguing
\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\gtrsim\eta_{J})=0
for all
J\leq 0
since
\eta_{J}\gtrsim d_{m}
. But since our set is unbounded now, we no longer have this fact, so our proof becomes considerably more complex. Moreover, we consider cases for whether
S=\varnothing
and apply the law of total expectation for our final bound. We will show the contribution from the “trivial” part of the estimator (when
S=\varnothing
) is
\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}
, and in the subsequent theorem, demonstrate this won’t affect the minimax rate.

Lemma D.2
.

Consider the Gaussian noise setting. Let
\eta_{J}
be defined as in Theorem
5.9
. Suppose
\tilde{J}
is such (
5.4
) holds and also
\frac{d_{m}}{2^{\tilde{J}-1}(C+1)}\geq C_{1}(\kappa)\epsilon\sigma
. Then if
\nu^{\ast\ast}
denotes the output after at least
J^{\ast}
iterations of Algorithm
2
in the
S(R)\neq\varnothing
case or the smallest lexicographic point in
S(\hat{R})
in the
S(R)=\varnothing
case, we have for some constant
C_{9}(\kappa)
depending on
C
and
\kappa
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}\lesssim\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}+\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\exp\left(-\tfrac{NC_{9}(\kappa)\eta_{\tilde{J}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)+\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{N}.

Proof.

The proof is split up into six parts. In Parts I-III, we bound
\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\gtrsim y|S(R)\neq\varnothing)
for all
y\geq\eta_{\tilde{J}}
, which we do by separately considering cases where
y\gtrsim R
and
\eta_{\tilde{J}}\leq y\lesssim R
and stitching together the results. In Part IV, we deduce a bound on
\mathbb{E}_{X}[\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}|S\neq\varnothing]
. Part V considers the
S(R)=\varnothing
scenario, in which case we set the estimator to be
\hat{p}\in S(\hat{R})
(defined in the main text). For any suitable
k\in\mathbb{N}
we bound
\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{p}-\mu\|^{2}|2^{k-1}R<\hat{R}\leq 2^{k}R]
as well as
\mathbb{P}(2^{k-1}R<\hat{R}\leq 2^{k}R)
. Applying the law of total expectation in Part VI will let us bound the unconditional risk
\mathbb{E}_{X}\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|^{2}
using our conditional risk bounds.

Part I: Bounding
\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\gtrsim t|S\neq\varnothing)
for
t\gtrsim R
.

Let
C_{6}(\kappa)
be defined as in Lemma
D.1
. Lemma
D.1
proved for
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
that

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Upsilon_{J}-\mu\|>\tfrac{d_{m}}{2^{J-1}}|S\neq\varnothing\right)\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{NC_{6}(\kappa)\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)+4\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Suppose
S\neq\varnothing
. Recall that we had a
2m
-covering of
K
(hence
S
) and
\Upsilon_{1}
is the closest point in that covering to
S
(using lexicographic ordering to break ties). Hence picking any
p\in S(R)
, we have

\|\Upsilon_{1}-\mu\|\leq\|\Upsilon_{1}-p\|+\|p-\mu\|\leq(2m+2R)+\|p-\mu\|,

(D.1)

using
\mathrm{diam}(S(R))\leq 2R
and the fact
\Upsilon_{1}
is from a
2m
-covering to argue
\|\Upsilon_{1}-p\|\leq 2m+2R
. Then for
t\geq R
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|p-\mu\|>2t|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mu\not\in S(t),S\neq\varnothing)}{\mathbb{P}(S\neq\varnothing)}\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mu\not\in S(t))}{\mathbb{P}(S\neq\varnothing)}

(D.2)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma t^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right).

The first inequality follows since
p\in S(R)
implies
p\in S(t)
using Lemma
5.4
, but both
p
and
\mu
cannot belong to
S(t)
if
\|p-\mu\|>2t
; otherwise, it would violate
\mathrm{diam}(S(t))\leq 2t
(Lemma
5.2
). We also used expanded the conditional probability. The second inequality dropped the intersection and the third applied Lemma
5.3
. We also used that
\mathbb{P}(S\neq\varnothing)>1/2
, as we proved in Lemma
D.1
using our third requirement on
R
in (
5.1
). Hence for
t\geq R
, (
D.1
) and (
D.2
) imply

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\Upsilon_{1}-\mu\|>2t+2m+2R|S\neq\varnothing)\leq 2\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma t^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right).

Recall
\|\nu^{\ast\ast}-\Upsilon_{1}\|\leq\frac{(2m+2R)(1+2c)}{c}
by our Cauchy sequence result (applying Lemma
5.8
). By the triangle inequality, for
t\geq R

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|>2t+2m+2R+\tfrac{(2m+2R)(1+2c)}{c}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma t^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right).

Recall we took
m=\tfrac{R}{c-1}
, so
2m+2R=\tfrac{2c}{c-1}\cdot R\leq\frac{c}{c-1}\cdot 2t
. Hence

2t+2m+2R+\tfrac{(2m+2R)(1+2c)}{c}\leq 2t\left(1+\tfrac{c}{c-1}\left(1+\tfrac{1+2c}{c}\right)\right)=t\cdot\tfrac{8c}{c-1}.

Set
C_{8}=\tfrac{8c}{c-1}=\tfrac{16(C+1)}{2C+1}
. Hence for
t\geq R
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|>C_{8}t|S\neq\varnothing)\leq 2\exp\left(-\tfrac{N(1/2-\epsilon)\gamma t^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\kappa\gamma t^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}\right).

(D.3)

We used
1/2-\epsilon>\kappa
in the last inequality.

Part II: Bounding
\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\gtrsim t|S\neq\varnothing)
for
\eta_{\tilde{J}}\leq t\lesssim R
.

Now we prove a similar bound for
t\lesssim R
by re-using logic from the bounded case. That is, repeat the proof of Lemma
B.7
through (
B.12
) with our modified bounds and conclude for
1\leq J\leq\tilde{J}
that

\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>\omega\eta_{J}|S\neq\varnothing)\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{NC_{6}(\kappa)\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)+4\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right),

where
\omega=\tfrac{7+6C}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}
and
\nu^{\ast}
is
\Upsilon_{J^{\ast}}
(the output of
J^{\ast}-1
steps).

Observe that
\bigcup_{1<J\leq\tilde{J}}[\eta_{J},\eta_{J-1})=[\eta_{\tilde{J}},\eta_{1})
and
\eta_{J}=\eta_{J-1}/2
. Moreover, any
\eta_{\tilde{J}}\leq x<\eta_{1}
belongs to some interval
[\eta_{J},\eta_{J-1})
for
1<J\leq\tilde{J}
and therefore satisfies
2\omega x\geq 2\omega\eta_{J}=\omega\eta_{J-1}
. Hence for
x\in[\eta_{\tilde{J}},\eta_{1})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>2\omega x|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>\omega\eta_{J-1}|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{NC_{6}(\kappa)\eta_{J}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)+4\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\eta_{J-1}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{NC_{6}(\kappa)x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)+4\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp\left(-\tfrac{Nx^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Let
\nu^{\ast\ast}=\Upsilon_{J^{\ast}+1}
be the output of
J^{\ast\ast}\geq J^{\ast}
steps. In the lines leading up to (
B.14
), we showed
\|\nu^{\ast}-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\leq\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\omega x
since
x\geq\eta_{\tilde{J}}
. This implies by the triangle inequality
\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\leq\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|+\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\omega x
for such
x
. Setting
\omega^{\prime}=\left(2+\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\right)\omega=\tfrac{19+16C}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}
, we obtain for
x\in[\eta_{\tilde{J}},\eta_{1})
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|>\omega^{\prime}x|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|+\tfrac{5+4C}{7+6C}\omega x>\omega^{\prime}x|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast}\|>2\omega x|S\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{NC_{6}(\kappa)x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)+4\cdot\mathbbm{1}(J>1)\exp\left(-\tfrac{Nx^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

(D.4)

Part III: Bounding
\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|\gtrsim t|S\neq\varnothing)
for all
t\geq\eta_{\tilde{J}}
.

We wish to combine our two bounds in (
D.3
) and (
D.4
), so let us compare
C_{8}R
and
\omega^{\prime}\eta_{1}
, which is the smallest and largest respective deviation for
\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|
controlled by either bound. Well, recalling
d_{m}=2m+2R=\tfrac{2c}{c-1}\cdot R=\tfrac{4(C+1)}{2C+1}\cdot R
, observe

\omega^{\prime}\eta_{1}=\tfrac{19+16C}{2\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}\cdot\tfrac{d_{m}\sqrt{C_{3}(\kappa)}}{(C+1)}=\tfrac{19+16C}{2(C+1)}\cdot(2m+2R)=\tfrac{2(19+16C)}{2C+1}\cdot R,

while
C_{8}R=\tfrac{16(C+1)}{2C+1}\cdot R.
Thus, one can see that
\omega^{\prime}\eta_{1}>C_{8}R
.

Now, if we set
y=C_{8}t/\omega^{\prime}
in (
D.3
), we obtain an equivalent bound for all
y\geq C_{8}R/\omega^{\prime}
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\mu-\nu^{\ast\ast}\|>\omega^{\prime}y|S\neq\varnothing)\leq 2\exp\left(-\tfrac{N\kappa{\omega^{\prime}}^{2}\gamma y^{2}}{32C_{8}^{2}\sigma^{2}}\right).

(D.5)

But this bound clearly holds for
y\geq\eta_{1}
since
\eta_{1}\geq C_{8}R/\omega^{\prime}
